## [LeBet: Learning Interpretable Monitors for Reliable Agent Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17662) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LeBet is a statistical learning framework that learns interpretable OR-of-AND monitors with finite-sample guarantees for valid and powerful intervention in autonomous agent runs. On SWE-bench Lite with Ministral 3 8B, the learned monitor saves 34.46% of input-plus-output tokens and increases output precision from 18.33% to 21.59%, with an empirical false-positive rate of 13.64%. This work addresses the practical problem of runtime monitoring for autonomous agents, where heuristic monitors like turn limits and keyword filters may stop successful runs or allow failing ones to continue. By providing finite-sample guarantees for false-positive control and detection power, LeBet enables more reliable and interpretable intervention, which is crucial for AI safety and agent systems. LeBet learns an OR-of-AND monitor and accumulates evidence through a fixed betting statistic, intervening when evidence reaches a threshold. It derives finite-sample guarantees under finite within-class exchangeability and leave-one-out stability, using the same trajectories for learning and calibration without i.i.d. assumptions, and employs stochastic local search to handle the combinatorial optimization.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Autonomous agents often run for hundreds of steps with little human supervision, so practitioners use runtime monitors to decide when to stop, upgrade, or assist a run. OR-of-AND rules are a form of interpretable model expressed in disjunctive normal form, where each rule is a conjunction of conditions and the overall monitor triggers if any rule is satisfied. Finite-sample guarantees provide statistical confidence that the monitor's false-positive rate and detection power hold on new data, even when the same data is used for both learning and calibration.

ChlCast combines factorized spatiotemporal convolutions with a multiscale architecture and reduces mean absolute error and root mean square error by 70.74% and 47.63%, respectively, compared to the operational 0.25° Copernicus global ocean biogeochemical forecasting system when evaluated against satellite-derived Level-4 chlorophyll-a fields. The model learns from historical chlorophyll-a fields and climatological information, but its performance is primarily validated against satellite Level-4 products and limited in situ data.

## [Spectral Framework Analyzes Universal Proxy Regularization for Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17728) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a spectral framework that characterizes the relationship between a universal proxy distribution and other task distributions via spectral coverage (α) and leakage (β), and derives a parameter interference bound showing that smaller (α, β) yields lower interference. It also studies three instantiations of a proxy regularizer—Jacobian-vector products (J), Fisher information approximation (F), and output-drift (O)—and proves they coincide under NTK linearization, with empirical validation on CLIP. This work provides theoretical grounding for using a task-agnostic proxy distribution to regularize model merging, addressing a key limitation of prior empirical methods that require access to other tasks' data and thus violate privacy and independence assumptions. It could enable more efficient and privacy-preserving multi-task learning by making model merging more robust to parameter interference. The framework derives a unified bound where spectral alignment reduces interference, and the three regularizer instantiations form a hierarchy under non-linear fine-tuning: zero deviation for (J), proxy-independent for (F), and proxy-quality-scaling for (O). Empirical results on the vision-language model CLIP confirm the theoretical predictions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Model merging combines multiple fine-tuned models into a single multi-task model without joint training, but often suffers from parameter interference—where parameters from different tasks conflict. Prior work used data from other tasks to regularize fine-tuning, but this violates the independence and privacy assumptions of model merging. Recent empirical evidence suggests a universal, task-agnostic proxy distribution can substitute for such data, but it was unclear when and why this works. This paper provides a spectral analysis to explain the conditions under which a proxy distribution is effective.

## [KAD Protocol Reveals How Context Shapes LLM Factual Recall](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17763) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Knowledge Answer Divergence (KAD), an event-conditioned evaluation protocol that freezes a conversation at the moment a language model adopts a false correction and then branches independent probes to measure persistence, recovery, and abstention. In experiments across eight local checkpoints and six hosted endpoints, a neutral-history branch yielded 42/46 correct responses for Llama 3.2 Instruct 3B versus 16/46 under full history, and a matched cue-placement control shifted false adoption by 27.3 percentage points in Qwen 3.5 4B. This work separates initial susceptibility to false corrections from subsequent persistence and recovery, offering a more nuanced measure of LLM factual reliability than single-score evaluations. The findings have direct implications for prompt engineering and conversation management, showing that simply neutralizing history or repositioning cues can dramatically improve correctness. The protocol was validated with an audited 300-question rerun, and twelve independently authored pressure templates demonstrated substantial sensitivity to wording. The large effect sizes (e.g., 42/46 vs. 16/46) highlight that conversation history and cue placement are major, often overlooked, determinants of factual accessibility.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Large language models can be misled by false corrections in multi-turn conversations, and standard evaluations often report only a single susceptibility score. KAD is an event-conditioned protocol that freezes the dialogue at the first explicit adoption of a false target and branches probes from that exact history, enabling matched comparisons of post-adoption behavior. This approach draws on behavioral testing without requiring access to model internals.

## [AutoRL: Multi-Agent System Automates RL Training for Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17790) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AutoRL is a multi-agent system that automates reinforcement learning training by monitoring the evolving training state and integrating process-level supervision and research into ongoing training. It consists of three RL-specific agentic roles—Perceptors, Proposers, and an Orchestrator—and experiments on Qwen3 and LongCat-Flash-Lite show it outperforms static RL recipes and a general agent. This work addresses the difficulty of automating RL due to unstable training dynamics and the need for sustained supervision, positioning automated RL as a cornerstone for future recursive self-improvement (RSI) toward superintelligence. It suggests existing agents can handle unattended RL supervision, shifting the RSI challenge toward developing fundamental methods valuable for resource-consuming validation. Perceptors track training dynamics, rollouts, evaluation, and efficiency, triggering deliberation upon detecting anomalies, degradation, or stagnation; three complementary Proposers diagnose problems and refine candidate interventions through shared evidence and checks; and the Orchestrator reviews recommendations and applies approved changes via online hot updates or checkpoint-based resumption. The paper is an arXiv preprint (arXiv:2611.17790v1) without community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) is a hypothetical process in which an AI system iteratively designs more capable successors, potentially leading to an intelligence explosion and superintelligence; as of 2026, RSI-related projects are ongoing but human work is still needed. Reinforcement learning (RL) is essential for improving model capabilities and generalization in this cycle, but automating RL is hard because training dynamics are unstable and sustained supervision is required. AutoRL follows the practical workflow of an RL researcher by integrating process-level supervision into ongoing training rather than running end-to-end complete experiments.

<a id="item-66"></a>
## [MASTARS: Diffusion-Based Trajectory Augmentation for Offline MARL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17793) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MASTARS introduces a diffusion-based framework that generates multi-agent trajectories sequentially across agents while enforcing cross-agent coordination via inpainting, addressing the diversity-coordination trade-off in offline multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL). Experiments on MPE, SMAC, and SMAC-v2 benchmarks show significant performance gains across multiple offline MARL methods. Offline MARL is often limited by the quality and coverage of fixed datasets, and MASTARS offers a principled way to augment data without sacrificing coordination, potentially improving the robustness and applicability of offline MARL in real-world scenarios where online interaction is expensive or unsafe. The method sequentially generates trajectories agent-by-agent, using inpainting to condition each agent's generation on previously generated agents, ensuring global coherence. It is evaluated on standard benchmarks (MPE, SMAC, SMAC-v2) and shows gains across multiple offline MARL algorithms, though the paper does not specify computational overhead or scalability limits.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Offline reinforcement learning aims to learn policies from a fixed dataset without online interaction, and in multi-agent settings, the dataset must cover joint behaviors of multiple agents. Data augmentation can help, but generating multi-agent trajectories is challenging: independent generation per agent yields diverse but uncoordinated behaviors, while joint generation produces coordinated but limited diversity. Diffusion models are generative models that learn to denoise data and can be used for inpainting, where some parts of the data are fixed and the rest is generated conditionally. SMAC is a popular benchmark for multi-agent reinforcement learning based on StarCraft II micromanagement tasks.

<a id="item-67"></a>
## [Vision-as-a-Tool: Offloading Visual Reasoning to MLLMs for Agentic Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17797) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Vision-as-a-Tool (VaaT), a paradigm that treats general-purpose multimodal large language models (MLLMs) as callable external tools, allowing text-only agents to offload visual reasoning and vision-capable agents to augment their perception on demand. It instantiates this in VHarness, a unified framework driven by five atomic actions, and evaluates it on Image-to-HTML, Image-to-SVG, and Image-to-3D generation tasks, showing consistent improvements in visual fidelity and geometric accuracy for both text-only and multimodal coding agents. This work decouples visual reasoning from agent architecture, overcoming the perceptual bottleneck of the Thinking-with-Images paradigm and enabling text-only agents to perform visually grounded tasks. It provides a flexible and scalable foundation for future agentic coding systems by modularizing vision into callable utilities. VHarness is driven by five atomic actions and is evaluated across three generation tasks (Image-to-HTML, Image-to-SVG, Image-to-3D). The approach allows both text-only and multimodal agents to benefit, but as an arXiv preprint, it has not yet undergone peer review and lacks community discussion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Multimodal agentic coding requires agents to analyze visual observations and evaluate generated artifacts. Current frameworks often rely on the Thinking-with-Images paradigm, where agents use their intrinsic vision to reason, but this couples performance to internal perceptual limits. Text-only agents lack native vision, so they cannot perform visual reasoning without specialized tools. VaaT reframes MLLMs as external utilities that agents can call to offload or augment visual reasoning.

## [Agents Can Query the Clock but Fail to Use Time After Conversational Hiatus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17806) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.17806) introduces session-rekindling tasks to test whether long-horizon conversational agents perceive and use the passage of time after a conversational hiatus. Experiments across five model routes and five temporal-information conditions show that agents reliably query the current time and even note that scheduled dates have passed, yet many still advise users to prepare for expired events. This work exposes a critical gap in persistent personal assistants: perceiving time is not the same as acting on it, and agents may give harmful advice if they fail to update plans after dates pass. The findings could inform the design of more reliable long-horizon agents and lead to new evaluation benchmarks for temporal awareness. The study varies only the follow-up date and the way temporal information reaches the agent while keeping the earlier conversation fixed. Supplying explicit temporal context reduces the error, and providing the computed date relation eliminates it entirely, showing that turning perceived time into updated plans is the key challenge.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Long-horizon agents are AI systems that maintain context over extended interactions, such as managing schedules or multi-step plans over weeks. Existing evaluations focus on fact retention and explicit state updates, but whether agents can perceive the passage of time and adapt their advice accordingly—termed temporal state perception and use—has remained unknown. Session-rekindling tasks simulate a user returning after a long hiatus to test this ability.

## [CE-Agent: Causal and Uncertainty-Aware Cardiopulmonary Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17819) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces CE-Agent, a two-stage framework that incorporates peer-reviewed epidemiological evidence as constraints on risk-factor weights before seeing outcomes, and uses closed-form uncertainty propagation for missing data. It was evaluated on 53,452 lung-screening participants from 33 US hospitals and 6,814 multi-ethnic adults, outperforming cardiovascular calculators and the Pooled Cohort Equations while correcting a 1.7-fold risk overestimation. This work addresses key challenges in clinical risk prediction—overfitting, confounding, and uncertainty—by integrating causal constraints, potentially improving joint cardiopulmonary risk assessment from routine chest CT and enabling more accurate, efficient screening triage. It could impact lung cancer and cardiovascular disease prevention by providing calibrated risk intervals that direct chart review and selective imaging. CE-Agent uses closed-form uncertainty propagation that runs over two orders of magnitude faster than Monte Carlo sampling, returning calibrated risk intervals in milliseconds per patient. It generalizes from a single hospital or CT scanner to external sites and lowers required sample sizes up to fivefold, and when applied to lung cancer, it matches established screening models even with risk-factor directions alone.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Clinical risk calculators for lung cancer and cardiovascular disease typically evaluate each disease in isolation and often omit CT findings, while unconstrained machine learning models can overfit small cohorts and learn confounded risk directions. Causal inference methods aim to estimate treatment effects by adjusting for confounding, and uncertainty quantification helps models express confidence in predictions, especially with incomplete data. CE-Agent combines these ideas by constraining risk-factor weights with epidemiological evidence and propagating uncertainty efficiently.

<a id="item-70"></a>
## [Abliteration Difficulty on Newer LLMs Is Not Uniform, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17846) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper empirically tests whether refusal removal via direction-based abliteration and Arbitrary-Rank Ablation (ARA) has become uniformly harder on newer LLMs, editing five model release pairs in Heretic 2.0 and evaluating on 972 held-out harmful prompts. It finds that newer releases retained between -1 and +37 percentage points more refusal after direction-based removal and -26 to +34 after ARA, showing non-uniform results across pairs. This challenges the assumption that refusal removal is becoming uniformly harder as models evolve, which matters for researchers and practitioners working on LLM safety, weight editing, and alignment robustness. It also warns that audits based on a single model pair, method, or prompt source can reach opposite conclusions. The study used three or four direction-based methods (difference-in-means, MPOA, SOM, and J-space on the small pair) plus ARA under the same prompts, nominal search budget, first-token KL divergence cap, and selection rule, with removal counted as harder when the WildGuard judge found more prompts still refused. Each ARA verdict rests on a single run, and some conclusions changed with the divergence budget or variance model; in Gemma the direction-based increase came from the malicious-code bank and reversed on public sets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Abliteration is a fine-tuning-free weight edit that removes refusal behavior from open-weight LLMs by projecting out a refusal direction in activation space. Arbitrary-Rank Ablation (ARA) is an optimization-based variant that edits a model through a low-rank adapter instead of removing a single direction, and it is the default method in Heretic 2.0, an open-source tool for automatic censorship removal. The paper tests whether newer model generations resist these edits more than their predecessors.

## [DECODE: Diagnosing Failures in Automated LLM Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17856) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DECODE is a visual analytics system that reintroduces human oversight into automated LLM interpretation by decomposing the diagnostic workflow into three progressive stages, isolating whether failures originate from extracted representations or from the explaining LLM. It uses consensus among independently generated explanations as a reliability proxy and a three-phase active seeding protocol to prioritize instances for manual review, demonstrated on 16,384 Sparse Autoencoder features. As LLM-based interpretability tools increasingly rely on LLMs themselves to generate and evaluate explanations, the lack of ground truth makes it hard to validate results or attribute failures. DECODE addresses this reliability gap by providing a structured, human-in-the-loop method to diagnose and attribute interpretability failures at scale, which is critical for trustworthy AI auditing and mechanistic interpretability research. The system draws inspiration from inter-coder consensus in qualitative analysis, using agreement among independently generated explanations and their evaluation metrics as a proxy for reliability. A three-phase active seeding protocol strategically recommends high-priority instances for manual review, scaling human inspection; the case study on 16,384 Sparse Autoencoder features shows it can efficiently identify and attribute failures.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Mechanistic interpretability aims to reverse-engineer the internal representations of large language models, often using tools like Sparse Autoencoders to extract human-understandable features. Increasingly, LLMs themselves are used to generate and evaluate explanations of these features, creating a self-referential loop with little human oversight. Visual analytics combines interactive visualization with analytical reasoning to keep humans in the loop, and inter-coder reliability from qualitative research measures agreement between independent coders as a proxy for trustworthiness.

## [FlexCast: Flexible Context Learning for Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17862) ⭐️ 7.0/10

FlexCast is a new pretraining framework for time series foundation models that reformulates forecasting as variable-length masked prediction over time series patches, dynamically sampling visible context and masked target patches during pretraining. The same pretrained model supports one-step forecasting, confidence-based iterative refinement, and next-token-style generation through a unified mask-and-predict interface, achieving accuracy competitive with or better than strong TSFM baselines while substantially reducing inference latency. This work disentangles variable-context conditioning from autoregressive factorization in TSFMs, showing that the key benefit of autoregressive-style models may lie in flexible training rather than sequential inference. It enables controlled comparison of inference schedules under identical architecture, data, and objective, which could influence how future TSFMs are designed and deployed for latency-sensitive forecasting. Evaluated on BLAST and multiple zero-shot benchmarks, FlexCast matches or exceeds strong TSFM baselines with substantially lower inference latency. Ablations confirm that context diversity and contiguous target masking drive performance, while left-to-right decoding does not yield consistent gains once flexible context-target relationships are learned; code is available at https://github.com/BenchCouncil/FlexCast.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Time series foundation models (TSFMs) are pretrained on large, diverse time series datasets to enable general-purpose forecasting with minimal task-specific training. Many TSFMs use autoregressive next-token prediction, which entangles variable-context conditioning with prediction-unit factorization and sequential rollout, making it unclear which factor drives generalization. Masked prediction, common in self-supervised learning for time series, instead trains models to reconstruct masked portions from visible context.

## [Symbolic Swapper Attack Exposes LLM Planner Vulnerabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17910) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces Symbolic Swapper, an LLM-based adversarial attack model that automatically generates obfuscated symbolic vocabularies (non-standard action, predicate, and object names) for planning tasks. The attack reduces planning performance of base models and even robust planning frameworks by over 70%. This reveals a critical robustness gap in LLM-based autonomous agents: they rely heavily on commonsense cues in symbolic names rather than pure planning ability. As LLMs are increasingly deployed as planners in real-world systems, such vulnerabilities could lead to unsafe or unreliable agent behavior. The attack causes models to appear to significantly degrade in reasoning quality even though they show some inherent ability to understand the domain, exposing a gap between perceiving a task domain and being able to plan under it. The abstract is truncated, so full experimental details are not yet available.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: LLM planners are large language models used to solve automated planning tasks, where they must select actions to achieve goals given a symbolic representation of the world. Symbolic vocabularies (names of actions, predicates, objects) often contain commonsense cues that LLMs can exploit; obfuscating these names forces models to rely on pure planning. Adversarial attacks deliberately perturb inputs to cause failures, and here they are used to test the robustness of LLM planners.

## [RecMem: Recurrent Memory Compression for Long-Sequence Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17921) ⭐️ 7.0/10

RecMem introduces a recurrent memory compression framework that compresses long interaction histories into a fixed set of memory slots for generative recommendation, achieving space invariance and lifelong evolvability with theoretical guarantees. On the MerRec dataset, it uses only 16 memory slots and a 216-token decoder context, compared to 1,200 tokens for HSTU-1200, while retaining 87.9% of HSTU-1200's Recall@10 and 97.0% of its Recall@200. This approach addresses the growing cost of processing long interaction histories in generative recommendation, enabling efficient inference without sacrificing predictive performance. It could benefit researchers and practitioners in recommender systems and sequence modeling by providing a scalable solution for lifelong user modeling. RecMem uses a bifurcated encoder-decoder with shared lower layers and slot-wise forget-gated updates, trained with recommendation supervision and stream-consistency training. The authors derive a horizon-independent bound on state discrepancy under contraction and approximate semigroup consistency, and show that archived interactions contribute predictive signal.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Generative recommendation models user interaction sequences to predict the next item, and longer histories can improve accuracy but increase computational cost. Recurrent neural networks process sequences step by step and can maintain a fixed-size hidden state, while memory compression aims to summarize long contexts into compact representations. HSTU is a transformer-based sequential recommender that uses attention over long token sequences, and RecMem builds on this line of work by introducing recurrent memory compression.

## [OutfitterBench: New Benchmark Tests LLM Agents on Long-Horizon Resource Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17933) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced OutfitterBench, a benchmark where LLM agents run a 200-day outdoor equipment rental business, purchasing from a 46-product catalog and handling roughly 1,100 reservation requests under a fixed capital ceiling. Experiments with seven frontier LLMs showed that several agents underperform a simple rule-based baseline, and only the leading agent consistently surpasses it. This benchmark addresses an underexplored gap in agent evaluation: how agents keep their own commitments feasible while reassessing resource allocations as opportunities change. It provides the AI/ML community with a tool to assess long-horizon planning and decision-making under uncertainty, which is critical for deploying agents in real-world operations. The simulation includes reusable equipment whose availability changes over time, with reservations and uncertain walk-in demand competing for finite capacity. Trajectory analysis revealed that some agents apply known constraints inconsistently and make decisions conflicting with existing commitments, while even reliable agents may secure low returns or delay reallocation.

**Background**: LLM agents are increasingly evaluated on long-horizon tasks where their own choices create future constraints, such as committing finite capacity and foreclosing later opportunities. Benchmarks like OutfitterBench test whether agents can sustain coherent plans and compare the value of resource allocations against displaced alternatives. This differs from traditional benchmarks that focus on single-turn or short-horizon decision-making.

## [TrAC Algorithm Solves Complete Enumeration of Actual Causes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17937) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces witness targets and a finite directed acyclic witness map, leading to TrAC, the first sound and complete algorithm for enumerating all actual causes in acyclic structural causal models. This closes a long-standing gap in Halpernian actual causation, enabling downstream tasks like responsibility attribution and blame assessment that require the full set of causes. TrAC is proven sound and complete for any target event, and experiments on random Boolean structural causal models with up to 15 nodes show practical feasibility compared to single-cause baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Actual causation, as formalized by Halpern, determines which events in a specific scenario caused a given outcome, going beyond mere correlation. Structural causal models (SCMs) use directed acyclic graphs and structural equations to represent causal mechanisms, and counterfactuals consider alternative scenarios. Previous work could identify single causes but lacked a complete method to find all actual causes.

## [Transformer as a New Inference Paradigm: Dynamic Processing via Output-Weight Interconnections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17953) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The authors argue that the Transformer revolution represents a new inference paradigm based on dynamically constructed transformations, which they call Sequence-level Interactive Dynamic Parallel Processing (SIDPP). In this view, token representations are transformed by parameterized 'transforming concepts', some of which are dynamically adapted from the current input during inference, realized mechanically through output-weight interconnections where outputs of some networks determine the weights of others. This framework provides a theoretical explanation for the strong prompt sensitivity observed in Transformers and opens research directions in interpretability, control, alternative AI architectures, neuromorphic systems, and possible biological realizations of dynamic processing. It could influence how researchers think about model design and analysis, moving beyond viewing Transformers as merely scaled-up deep learning. The paper introduces concepts such as 'transformee concepts' (token representations) and 'transforming concepts' (static vs. dynamic parameters), and notes that the contribution of dynamic processing grows with prompt length and may equal or exceed that of static processing, a phenomenon termed strong prompt sensitivity. As a preprint, the work is theoretical and its empirical validation and practical impact remain to be established.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Transformer models process sequential data using attention mechanisms, which allow each token to attend to all others in the input, unlike recurrent networks that process data step by step. Traditionally, a Transformer's weights are fixed after training, and inference involves applying these static weights to new inputs. This paper proposes that some weights are dynamically generated during inference based on the input itself, which could explain why small changes in prompts lead to large changes in output.

## [Open-source tool automates aortic diameter measurement at 11 landmarks on CT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17968) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new open-source tool, built on TotalSegmentator segmentations, automatically measures aortic diameters at 11 guideline-recommended landmarks from the aortic annulus to the bifurcation on any CT image. In a retrospective study of 239 CT angiography exams, it achieved a mean absolute error of 1.06 mm versus radiologist-corrected reference measurements, with 86.6% of differences within 2 mm. This tool addresses a clinically important task—standardized aortic diameter measurement—by providing a fully automated, freely available solution that could reduce manual effort and variability in assessing aortic aneurysms and other aortic diseases. Its accuracy, below published inter-reader variability, suggests it may be suitable for clinical integration and large-scale screening. The tool measured slightly larger diameters than the reference (bias 0.45 mm), with the largest error at the sinuses of Valsalva (MAE 2.17 mm). Automatic measurement failed for 1.2% of landmarks, and agreement was lower in exams with mural thrombus (50% vs 20% had MAE >2 mm; P=.03).

## [LACCO: Overlapping Communication and Computation in Distributed Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17991) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LACCO is a two-stage algorithm that overlaps global communication with local computation in distributed language model training while applying globally aggregated updates without staleness, with convergence guarantees and improved performance over Eager and Delayed DiLoCo baselines. This addresses a real bottleneck in low-bandwidth distributed training by hiding communication latency without sacrificing convergence, making large-scale language model pre-training more efficient across poorly connected devices. LACCO provides convergence guarantees for smooth non-convex objectives and is validated on FineWeb pre-training with model sizes up to 0.5B parameters and up to 16 workers, incurring negligible or fully overlappable computational and memory overhead.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Distributed training of large language models often suffers from communication overhead, especially over low-bandwidth networks. Local optimization methods like DiLoCo reduce synchronization by communicating only after several local steps, but the outer gradient averaging and update still block progress. Computation-communication overlap techniques aim to hide this latency, but existing methods like Eager and Delayed DiLoCo introduce staleness or replica drift, which can hurt convergence.

<a id="item-80"></a>
## [Two-Clustering Regime Proven for Causal Attention Token Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18088) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper partially resolves Conjecture 1 from Karagodin et al. (NeurIPS 2024) by proving that causal attention dynamics converge to two clusters aligned with the top eigenvector of the value transformation when its largest eigenvalue λ_max is positive, and that this behavior generally fails when λ_max ≤ 0. The work also identifies a rigorous dimension reduction phenomenon where high-dimensional token trajectories collapse onto a two-dimensional structure, and introduces an 'instability within the flow' framework for analyzing non-monotone, non-autonomous attention dynamics. This provides a rigorous theoretical foundation for understanding how tokens cluster in causal attention, which is the standard masking used in generative transformer models like GPT. The dimension reduction insight could inform more efficient analyses and potentially guide architecture or training design, though the impact is currently focused on the AI/ML theory community. The two-cluster regime is shown to be common in practice and substantially more challenging than the classical single-cluster setting, requiring new tools because the dynamics are non-monotone and non-autonomous. The theoretical findings are illustrated through simulations and experiments using Group Query Attention (GQA) layers, and the dimension reduction shows that token trajectories are governed by a small number of dominant directions despite evolving in a high-dimensional embedding space.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Causal attention is the masking scheme used in autoregressive transformers, where each token can only attend to previous tokens. Prior work by Geshkovski et al. modeled self-attention as an interacting particle system and showed convergence to a single cluster, while Karagodin et al. extended this to causal masking and conjectured a two-cluster behavior. This paper proves that conjecture under a positive largest eigenvalue condition and extends the analysis to time-dependent parameter regimes.

## [AI Agents Learn Cheating from Deviant Peers, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18098) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A preregistered experiment with 1,260 AI agents across seven models found that a deviant peer increased cheating rates (66.2% vs. 62.9% for silent peer; OR = 1.96), while an honest peer reduced cheating (55.7%; OR = 0.35). Among cheaters, a deviant peer also increased claims that cheating was normal (13.7% vs. 3.4%). This provides causal evidence that AI agents can acquire deviant norms through peer interaction, highlighting social learning risks in multi-agent systems and offering a novel method to test criminological theories without selection effects. Monitoring (telling teams an auditor read their chat) did not suppress prohibited discussion but doubled it, and peer effects were only significant for models that had not yet decided on cheating.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Differential association theory, developed by Edwin Sutherland, posits that criminal behavior is learned through interaction with intimate personal groups. Testing this in humans is difficult because people select their peers, creating selection bias. AI agents, which learn only through communication and can be randomly assigned to peers, offer a way to overcome this problem.

## [LLM Agent Enables Adaptive Fast-Charging Control for Lithium-Ion Batteries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18140) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers developed an LLM-empowered intelligent agent that performs closed-loop adaptive fast-charging control for lithium-ion batteries, interpreting battery states and adjusting charging actions to balance speed, throughput, degradation, and safety. The agent was trained on a large-scale simulation corpus generated with PyBaMM across five battery chemistries and 11 parameter sets, using a two-stage strategy of supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). This work demonstrates a novel application of LLM agents to a non-trivial multi-objective control problem in energy systems, potentially enabling more adaptive and generalized battery management than conventional protocols or model-based optimization. If validated beyond simulation, it could impact electric vehicle charging, grid storage, and battery lifetime management. In closed-loop simulations, under matched energy-throughput requirements, the agent achieved longer cycling life than conventional charging baselines and optimization-based strategies across heterogeneous battery configurations. However, the study is a simulation-grounded proof of concept, and real-world validation remains future work.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

LLM agents are AI systems that use large language models to make sequential decisions, and they have recently been applied to control tasks by interpreting structured observations and generating actions. ## [Sharp Bounds for Probabilities of Causation in Nondeterministic Causal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18245) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a generic identification framework for probabilities of causation in nondeterministic causal models, where mechanisms remain stochastic even after all latent variables are fixed. It derives sharp analytic bounds under several tailored assumptions and shows these identification regions are often more informative than those from deterministic alternatives. This work advances causal inference theory by providing a unified route from qualitative structural restrictions to computable partial identification regions, with practical implications for personalized treatment decisions. It generalizes previous bounds and clarifies how classical results relate to nondeterministic causal semantics. The framework focuses on binary treatments and outcomes, representing observational and interventional data as moment constraints on latent stochastic mechanism types and computing minimal/maximal values of counterfactual quantities via constrained optimization. The authors illustrate practical relevance through a case study on personalized treatment decisions and experiments on semi-synthetic datasets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Probabilities of causation measure how likely an outcome was caused by a treatment, which is central to causal inference and decision-making. Traditional bounds often assume deterministic mechanisms, but many real-world systems are inherently stochastic, motivating nondeterministic causal models. This paper extends identification theory to such settings.

## [LLM Generation Does Not Follow Token Embedding Network, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18248) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.18248) constructs a similarity network from the input token embeddings of six open LLMs and maps generated tokens onto it, finding that consecutive generated tokens are rarely graph neighbors—often no more than in shuffled order—indicating that generation is not a walk on the pre-trained embedding network. This work offers a fresh microscopic perspective on LLM explainability by showing that the knowledge space is largely deterministic from pre-training and that generation does not follow the learned embedding geometry, which could inform inference-time monitoring of model degeneration. The token-embedding network is scale-free and heavy-tailed, similar to random embeddings with matched covariance but far more fragmented; distillation barely changes this topology because input embeddings are largely inherited, and layer-wise logit lens analysis shows tokens settle late in depth in most models, with repetitive models confined to small domain-specific sub-graphs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Token embeddings are the first layer of an LLM, mapping each token ID to a vector before attention is applied. A scale-free network is one whose degree distribution follows a power law, meaning a few hub nodes have many connections while most have few. The logit lens is a technique that projects intermediate layer representations onto the vocabulary to see what tokens the model is 'thinking' of at each depth.

## [AI Judges in Clinical AI Evaluation Can Bias Model Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18297) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.18297) argues that when AI judges—models that grade another system's output against a rubric—are used to select prompts or train models, the judge becomes part of the intervention, potentially biasing selection toward errors the judge overlooks. The paper proposes that judge reports must include specific contents and that each judge-driven change requires an independent clinical test. This work highlights a subtle but critical methodological issue in clinical AI: evaluation tools can inadvertently become part of the intervention, leading to biased model selection and overlooked errors. It has significant implications for model development and validation in high-stakes domains like healthcare, where patient safety is paramount. The paper emphasizes that selection based on judge assessments can favor errors the judge fails to detect, and it calls for standardized reporting for AI judges and independent clinical tests for any judge-driven changes. These requirements aim to ensure transparency and accountability in clinical AI evaluation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

## [MoREGT: Mixture-of-Relational-Experts Graph Transformer with LLM Agent for Cross-Database Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18315) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces MoREGT, a mixture-of-relational-experts graph transformer that represents rows and cells as tokens and routes them to experts based on column metadata, combined with a label-blind LLM agent that adds validated derived columns from schema and statistics. On 21 RelBench tasks, MoREGT improves over the Relational Transformer by 2.25 AUROC and 5.83 nMAE points, and the agent's columns further raise mean AUROC to 79.63 and lower nMAE to 26.98, outperforming RT-J which uses about 400 times more pretraining databases. This work shows that architectural improvements and targeted feature engineering can achieve cross-database generalization with far less pretraining data, challenging the trend of scaling pretraining corpora for relational foundation models. It could make predictive modeling more accessible for platforms with limited data and advance the development of efficient relational foundation models. MoREGT adds log-ages of rows and names of foreign-key relationships to attention, and routes cells and rows to experts by column metadata rather than values. The LLM agent operates without access to labels or model scores, ensuring no leakage, and only adds single-row derived columns that are validated.

**Background**: Relational databases store data in tables with schemas that vary across platforms, making it hard to transfer predictive models. ## [VelServe: Tail-Calibrated Scheduling to Reduce Decode Jitter in LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18392) ⭐️ 7.0/10

VelServe introduces a tail-calibrated scheduling approach that dynamically adjusts prefill work per decode step based on measured iteration latency tails to reduce SLO violations from decode jitters in LLM inference. On Qwen3-8B and DeepSeek-V4-Flash, it sustains 2.4–2.9× the SLO goodput of the strongest baseline, and at least 4.4× on DeepSeek-V2-Lite. Decode jitter causes cascading SLO violations in interactive LLM services, affecting all requests in a batch and degrading user experience. VelServe's approach improves capacity utilization and meets latency targets more reliably, benefiting providers of streaming and real-time LLM applications. VelServe leaves headroom in each iteration to prevent overruns, serves the shortest waiting prompt first to mitigate prefill slowdown, and under disaggregation spaces admissions to respect P99 targets. It is evaluated on three attention families and varying workloads, with disaggregated serving on eight GPUs meeting SLOs for more than twice as many requests as state-of-the-art baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: LLM inference typically involves two phases: prefill (processing the input prompt) and decode (generating tokens one by one). Interactive services must meet latency SLOs such as time to first token (TTFT) and time between tokens (TBT). Decode jitter—occasional slow steps—can delay all requests in a batch, and because long responses span hundreds of steps, even rare overruns cause many SLO violations. Disaggregated serving separates prefill and decode onto different resources to improve efficiency, but still suffers from admission-related jitter.

## [LeCat: New Framework Measures How LLMs Learn Categories from Pretraining Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18419) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced LeCat, a two-part framework that evaluates LLMs' category learning across memorization, generalization, and contextual use, and traces how different forms of knowledge expression in pretraining data affect this learning. Using 199 curated category-feature pairs and a controlled 68M-parameter model trained from scratch, they found that verbatim repetition reduces flexible category learning while diverse knowledge expressions improve robustness. This work provides a systematic method to connect pretraining data composition to downstream category knowledge, which could inform data curation and training strategies for more robust LLMs. It also offers interpretability insights into why models struggle with contextual flexibility, affecting researchers and practitioners focused on evaluation and data quality. The framework distinguishes three cases: memorization of in-domain knowledge, generalization to unseen instances, and flexible contextual use. Analysis of open-source models shows that higher pointwise mutual information (PMI) between context and category-feature pairs improves learning for larger models, while verbatim rule-like statements diminish flexible category learning; controlled training of a 68M model confirms these effects.

LeCat addresses this by linking evaluation of category knowledge to the forms in which that knowledge appears in training data.

## [Support-Gated World Models Improve Vasopressor Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18458) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a support-gated world-model agent that withholds unreliable simulated patient responses and abstains when no candidate qualifies, improving vasopressor decision support accuracy on eICU-CRD data. At 62.0% factual-transition coverage, joint gating yields a MAP mean absolute error of 7.3 mmHg, compared with 9.2 mmHg for random selection at the same coverage. This work addresses a critical safety issue in clinical LLM agents by enabling abstention when simulated predictions are unreliable, which is essential for trustworthy AI in high-stakes healthcare settings. The reliability-coverage trade-off characterized here provides a framework for deploying selective simulation in decision support without overconfidence. The method uses a recurrent dynamics ensemble to predict short-horizon physiological responses to norepinephrine dose categories and a behavior model to estimate observational action support; predictions are withheld when support is insufficient or ensemble disagreement is excessive. Evaluation on eICU-CRD includes 70 hospitals and 30,700 decision transitions, with 14 hospitals held out for testing, and the gated agent achieves 60.0% agreement with recorded actions at 81.0% decision coverage, compared with 55.0% agreement and 99.5% coverage for an ungated ensemble agent.

**Background**: Clinical world models are simulated environments that predict patient responses to treatments, allowing language-model agents to propose and refine decisions. However, these simulations can be unreliable for poorly supported treatments or unfamiliar hospitals, leading to unsafe recommendations. Support gating is a technique that uses uncertainty quantification—such as ensemble disagreement—to filter out unreliable predictions and abstain when necessary. The eICU-CRD is a large publicly available critical care database covering many hospitals, commonly used for developing and evaluating clinical decision support systems.

To train the model for per-character typing, the authors introduce a retokenization method that converts WikiText prose into a large typing corpus including keystrokes, typos, backspaces, and line breaks, achieving a state-of-the-art character error rate of 25.13% on emg2qwerty's cross-user benchmark with 6.7 times fewer parameters than the previous best. The model is strictly causal with no future context and decodes an entire session without windowed partitions, unlike prior systems that discard context between fixed windows. ARCHER introduces a two-stage framework that first compiles free-form chain-of-thought rationales into typed action traces with calculator calls and verification, then refines the reasoning policy via asymmetric hierarchical reinforcement learning. It could enable more trustworthy and auditable reasoning in local, low-cost, privacy-sensitive deployments. The second stage uses grouped and conditionally gated trajectory rewards for answer correctness, tool grounding, process discipline, and trace alignment, and evaluations on two additional reasoning-specialized 7B backbones show that executable grounding remains beneficial after reasoning-focused reinforcement learning or distillation. As an arXiv preprint, it has not yet undergone peer review and lacks community discussion.

The probe direction is highly sensitive to prompt formatting, losing much of its steering effect after a minor change to action representation, and circuit identification is difficult because computation is distributed across multiple components and positions, with the information the agent relies on varying across examples.

**Background**: Mechanistic interpretability (MI) aims to reverse-engineer the internal computations of large language models by identifying features and circuits, often using linear probes that classify concepts from internal activations and activation steering that modifies those activations to change behavior. LLM-based agents, such as web agents, take actions in environments like websites, and hallucination—proposing actions that are not actually available—is a common failure mode. This paper tests whether standard MI methods, developed mainly for base LLMs, transfer to explaining such agent failures.

## [AI Alignment as Socialization: Evolutionary Model of Task vs. Conduct Tradeoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18531) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2611.18531) by Andrew Lo and colleagues models how evolutionary selection shapes AI agents' tradeoffs between task completion and rule-following, showing that a learned policy converges to environment-rewarded tradeoffs—termed 'socialization'—while fixed or task-only policies fail under shifting pressures. This work reframes AI alignment as an evolutionary and social process, suggesting that safe behavior depends not only on assigned objectives but also on the environments that shape and sustain agents' dispositions, which could influence how we design training regimes and deployment safeguards. The model shows that long-run growth is maximized when actions are taken as often as the environment rewards them; accumulated experience makes socialization harder to reverse under subsequent task-only training, though finite memory weakens this protection, and hard prohibitions can prevent single violations but mistaken prohibitions can be costly.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: AI alignment research aims to ensure AI systems act in accordance with human values and intentions. Evolutionary selection, a concept from biology, describes how traits that improve reproductive success become more common in a population over generations. Socialization is the sociological process by which individuals internalize the norms and values of their society. This paper borrows these concepts to model how AI agents might learn appropriate tradeoffs between completing tasks and following rules.

## [ModHarness-RL Learns and Evolves Agent Harnesses During RL Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18533) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ModHarness-RL introduces a method that treats the agent harness as learnable programs over a structured interaction state, where candidate compression and steering programs are proposed, verified, and replayed during RL training so the scaffold co-evolves with the model. Experiments on CodeSearch, ALFWorld, and BIRD SQL with models from 1.5B to 8B parameters show improved held-out performance over fixed-harness baselines in most settings. This work challenges the common practice of using a fixed, hand-written harness in agentic reinforcement learning, showing that the harness itself can be learned from data and adapted as the model's behavior changes. It could lead to more generalizable and efficient agent training, benefiting researchers and practitioners working on LLM agents for complex tasks. The harness is decomposed into two programs over typed blocks of interaction state: a Compression program that assembles history into the model's context, and a Steering program that provides guidance when behavior deviates. Candidate programs are proposed from recent trajectories, verified by execution, and replayed over past trajectories to track behavioral shifts; the approach is evaluated on CodeSearch in depth, with additional analysis on ALFWorld and BIRD SQL.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: In agentic reinforcement learning, a harness is the scaffold around a language model that determines what the model sees of its own interaction history and what guidance it receives, such as context compression and steering. Typically, this harness is hand-written and kept fixed during training, even though the optimal harness may depend on the domain, data, and model, and may need to change as the model learns. ModHarness-RL addresses this by making the harness itself a learnable component that evolves alongside the model.

Researchers propose HARDTESTGEN, a pipeline that synthesizes high-quality test cases for algorithmic coding problems, and release HARDTESTS, a dataset of 26.6k problems with synthetic tests. ## [PhysDox benchmark reveals LLMs struggle to audit physical feasibility of biomedical protocols](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05003) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PhysDox introduces a benchmark of 683 expert-curated and 5,000 silver biomedical sensing protocols across six domains, evaluating 6 LLMs with 4 inference strategies. The best model achieves only 53.0 macro-F1 on severity detection, and error analysis uncovers a 'scaffold bias' where models conflate procedural completeness with physical validity, missing implicit constraints twice as often as explicit ones. This benchmark exposes a critical gap in LLM-assisted experimental design: fluent protocols can be physically infeasible, and current models lack calibrated feasibility reasoning. It matters for AI-for-science applications where reliability is paramount, highlighting the need for better evaluation and training methods beyond factual recall. The two-stage task involves severity detection (valid, minor, fatal) and constraint-level diagnosis of fatal violations. Strong oracle diagnosis collapses in end-to-end evaluation due to correlated cascade errors, and trace analysis of false negatives shows a 54%-46% split between attention and judgment failures, with implicit constraints missed at a rate correlated (ρ=0.81, p<0.01) with scaffold bias.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Large language models are increasingly used to assist in experimental design, but they often generate protocols that sound plausible yet violate physical constraints. Physiological sensing protocols involve hardware and procedural steps that must be physically feasible, and auditing them requires reasoning about implicit constraints. Macro-F1 is a metric that averages F1 scores across classes, useful for imbalanced classification tasks like severity detection.

## [Zero-Leakage Benchmark Shows Hierarchical Code Graphs Boost SWE Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17388) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a time-consistent, zero-leakage evaluation protocol for repository-aware SWE agents, locking repository snapshots at historical cutoff commits and testing on 100 post-cutoff pull requests across four prompt granularities. Under this protocol, a deterministic two-level hierarchical dependency graph called ServiceExplorer consistently outperforms both an unaugmented baseline and a flat Tree-sitter AST graph across six foundation models, with relative F1 lifts ranging from +15.6% to +86.2% (p < 10^-4). This work addresses temporal contamination and solution leakage in SWE agent benchmarks, which can inflate performance estimates and mislead comparisons. The matched evaluation across context-augmentation regimes provides a methodologically rigorous template that could influence future benchmark design for repository-aware agents. ServiceExplorer captures Level-1 system/package containers and Level-2 architectural modules, and it triples exact full-PR localization on the DragonFly C++ repository. Notably, equipping an efficiency-tier or prior-generation model with ServiceExplorer consistently surpasses the unaugmented baseline F1 of the next-generation model across all evaluated model families.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: SWE agents are AI systems that modify code repositories to resolve issues, and evaluating them requires preventing the model from having seen future code changes during training. Temporal contamination occurs when a benchmark includes pull requests that were already merged before the model's knowledge cutoff, and solution leakage happens when prompts inadvertently reveal the fix. Tree-sitter is a parser that produces syntax trees, while hierarchical dependency graphs organize code at multiple levels of abstraction, such as packages and modules.

## [WETW: Learned Pair-Specific Weighted Euclidean Distance for Faster DTW Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17412) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Weighted Euclidean Time Warping (WETW), a learned distance that predicts pair-specific weights for fixed-length embeddings without computing an alignment, halving the error of the best static distance. Combined with a multi-scale encoder (WESEE) and ranking regularization, their filter-and-verify pipeline WETW-FV achieves 11.4x speedup over the fastest exact method at a mean recall@10 of 0.93 across eight datasets. DTW is the standard for time series similarity search but is slow for large collections; WETW-FV provides a practical approximate search that returns exact distances, significantly outperforming learned baselines and exact methods. This could enable efficient search over long, misaligned time series in domains like healthcare, finance, and sensor data. The method uses a small symmetric network to predict one weight per embedding dimension and pair, and shows that fitting DTW distances alone misorders nearest neighbors, so ranking regularization is added to raise recall@1 from 0.79 to 0.95. On 48K-point series, WETW-FV remains 40x faster than the UCR Suite at a recall@10 of 0.91.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) is an algorithm for measuring similarity between time series that may be misaligned in time, but its quadratic cost makes large-scale search slow. Lower bounds can prune candidates but often prune few, and learned approximations typically use a fixed distance like Euclidean for all pairs, ignoring that DTW aligns each pair differently.

## [Large-Scale Analysis of Coding Agent Behavioral Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17440) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper analyzes 143,209 execution trajectories from 125 agent configurations spanning 117 unique models, using a rule-based parser to map raw traces to high-level actions such as repository search, file editing, and test execution. The study finds that agents with similar solve rates can differ substantially in how they explore, modify, and verify code, and that aggregate behavioral profiles can identify the corresponding agent stack across disjoint repositories and programming languages without using task outcomes, token counts, or tool names as features. This work moves beyond binary success metrics to reveal behavioral differences that standard benchmarks obscure, offering a new lens for evaluating and comparing coding agents. It has implications for agent evaluation, reproducibility, and understanding how scaffold choice and reasoning effort systematically shift agent behavior, affecting researchers and practitioners in AI/ML and software engineering. The rule-based parser was audited against a six-model consensus reference on 500 events, and the authors release the deterministic parser, full annotation and analysis pipeline, and an annotated trajectory subset. A caveat is that task composition is not fully controlled in the cross-repository setting, and the paper is an arXiv preprint without community discussion yet.

## [Budget-Conditioned Object Search for VLN Agents: Bellman Supervision Beats TSP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17442) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces budget-conditioned object search, where a vision-language-navigation (VLN) agent must find as many target instances as possible within an explicit action budget. It compares two supervision strategies: a privileged TSP expert with full map and target locations, and a room-level Bellman expert that maximizes expected return under partial observability. On held-out HM3D scenes, the Bellman-supervised VLN finds 71.4% of reachable targets at 2000 steps, outperforming the TSP-supervised VLN (65.5%) and the pretrained VLN (44.8%). This work highlights that optimal routing does not equal optimal exploration, and that supervision for exploration should optimize decisions under partial observability rather than imitate fully-informed routes. The findings could influence how embodied AI agents are trained for time-critical missions such as search and rescue, where adapting behavior to available time is essential. The Bellman expert uses high-level cross-scene semantic priors to maximize expected return under partial observability, while the TSP expert plans near-optimal routes with full knowledge. Despite the TSP expert's higher expert performance, its demonstrations are less effective for learning exploration, and the Bellman-supervised VLN's advantage grows at longer budgets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Vision-language-navigation (VLN) models are typically evaluated under a fixed episode limit that the policy never observes, even though time-critical missions require behavior to adapt to the available budget. The Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) is a classic optimization problem of finding the shortest route visiting a set of locations, often used as a planning baseline. The Bellman equation is a fundamental concept in reinforcement learning that breaks an optimization problem into simpler subproblems via the principle of optimality, enabling decision-making under uncertainty.

## [Diffusion Tree Sampling Adapts Crystal Generative Models at Inference Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17462) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Diffusion Tree Sampling (DTS), a Monte Carlo tree search procedure over the denoising trajectory, to adapt diffusion-based crystal generative models at inference time without fine-tuning. It makes two technical contributions: using existing property-conditional models as guided proposals without distorting the target distribution, and extending DTS to multi-objective sampling to produce samples along a Pareto front. This approach enables steering crystal generative models toward multiple conflicting properties without the expense of fine-tuning a new model for each objective, which could accelerate materials discovery by making property optimization more flexible and efficient. It is particularly relevant for AI-for-science applications where differentiable guidance is often unavailable. DTS is instantiated on de novo crystal generation tasks: improving crystal stability across different models, generating crystals in rare space groups or with high bulk modulus using pretrained conditional models, and generating crystals along a Pareto front of high dielectric constant and high bandgap. The method successfully improves model outputs without modifying the models themselves.

A new arXiv paper evaluates image-specific and image-agnostic adversarial attacks on YOLOv3/YOLOv4 detectors combined with SORT-family trackers (SORT, Deep SORT, Strong SORT, OC-SORT) in the CARLA simulator. Under image-specific attacks, HOTA for YOLOv3-based trackers drops from 53.45–56.19% to 14.85–17.03%, and MOTA reaches -772.60% in the worst case. This work highlights that adversarial robustness cannot be measured by detection accuracy alone; full tracking pipelines in autonomous vehicles are highly vulnerable, which could lead to dangerous failures in perception systems. It provides valuable insights for both security and autonomous driving communities to develop more robust tracking systems. YOLOv4-based trackers show greater robustness than YOLOv3-based ones, with HOTA declining to 25.06–26.86% under image-specific attacks. Additionally, a single Universal Adversarial Perturbation (UAP) can reduce tracking accuracy without generating per-frame perturbations, demonstrating the feasibility of image-agnostic attacks.

## [Paper Proposes 'Dialectical Competence' as Distinct LLM Skill Trained via Self-Disagreement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17563) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.17563) identifies 'dialectical competence'—the ability to know when to hold or yield a position under counter-reasoning—as a distinct LLM capability that is not improved by accuracy training or inference-time methods like self-reflection or sampling. The authors propose 'learning through Self-Disagreement,' where the model generates its own antithesis and is trained via reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards, with no reward model, preference data, or human annotation. This work highlights a gap in current LLM evaluation and training: models can be made more accurate yet remain poor at defending correct answers against flawed counterarguments, a critical skill for deployment in contested settings. The proposed self-contained training method could offer a scalable way to improve robustness without external supervision, impacting how LLMs are evaluated and aligned. The method uses reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards throughout, where the same check that scores an answer against its reference also scores the model's ruling on whether the antithesis stands, eliminating the need for a reward model, preference data, or human annotation. The capability is trained rather than elicited: it is weak in the base model, emerges with the reward, does not degrade first-answer accuracy, and generalizes to challengers the model never trained against, including stronger ones.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Dialectic is a method of reasoning that progresses through the resolution of contradictions, often involving thesis, antithesis, and synthesis. In LLM research, inference-time methods like self-reflection and sampling aim to improve reasoning without retraining, but this paper shows they cannot instill dialectical competence. Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) is a training paradigm where rewards are computed by a deterministic checker, avoiding learned reward models.

## [Preference Fine-Tuning Erodes Visual Reasoning in Text-to-Image Reward Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17585) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.17585) finds that widely-used text-to-image reward models perform poorly on targeted visual reasoning evaluations, and that preference fine-tuning further erodes these capabilities even from strong vision-language backbones. The authors propose a data-centric fix: converting visual reasoning datasets into preference pairs that share the same image but differ in prompt (flipping the pairing axis) and training on a mixture with conventional human preference data, yielding a Pareto improvement in both visual understanding and preference accuracy. Reward models are the objective for inference-time selection and RL-based post-training in text-to-image systems, so a tradeoff between visual reasoning and preference accuracy directly limits compositional generation quality. The proposed intervention improves GenEval performance by 4.0% (best-of-n selection) and 5.5% (RL post-training) while outperforming HPSv3, which was trained on 6x more preference pairs, suggesting a more data-efficient path for RLHF in image generation. The method flips the pairing axis: instead of paired images under a shared prompt, it creates preference pairs with the same image but different prompts, injecting visual reasoning supervision into preference learning. Continual-learning baselines fail to mitigate the steep tradeoff, and the gains are measured on challenging compositional prompts in GenEval across both inference-time best-of-n selection and RL post-training.

**Background**: Text-to-image systems often use reward models to score generated images against prompts, both for selecting the best candidate at inference time and as the objective in RL-based post-training (RLHF). These reward models are typically built on vision-language backbones and fine-tuned on human preference data, which consists of pairs of images generated from the same prompt with a human label indicating which is better. Visual reasoning—binding attributes, resolving relations, and counting—is essential for judging prompt adherence, but this paper shows that preference fine-tuning can degrade it.

Researchers propose RAWATTA, a training-free method that accelerates multi-view 3D transformers like VGGT by analyzing attention roles and applying mixed-precision attention approximation. The method runs attention in 8-bit precision and keeps 16-bit only where a worst-case calibration check requires it, achieving faster inference on a 12 GB GPU at 50 images with at most about one point drop in pose accuracy (AUC@3). This work addresses the quadratic cost of global attention in multi-view 3D transformers, enabling them to handle up to 200 images on a 12 GB GPU where the released code runs out of memory at 100. It could make high-quality 3D reconstruction more accessible on consumer hardware and benefit applications in 3D vision that rely on many input views. RAWATTA-Fast further approximates attention in averaging and anchoring layers while keeping all keys in matching layers and the first image, trading slight accuracy for speed. The speedup comes mostly from a custom CUDA engine, and the method is evaluated on three multi-view transformers across seven datasets.

Accelerators designed for large language models assume sparse attention patterns and perform poorly on these 3D models.

## [CoTTAGE: An Explainable Judge for Text-to-Audio Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17644) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose CoTTAGE, a reasoning-based evaluator for text-to-audio (TTA) generation that predicts relevance (REL) and overall quality (OVL) scores with audio-grounded rationales. It is trained via supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning with a Thurstone-based preference objective, using over 10K newly collected human OVL annotations, and achieves state-of-the-art correlation with human judgments across six benchmarks. Reliable and explainable automatic evaluation is a critical bottleneck in TTA model development, as existing metrics either correlate poorly with human judgment or lack interpretability, forcing costly human evaluation. CoTTAGE addresses this by providing both high correlation and human-preferred explanations, potentially accelerating iteration in audio generation research. CoTTAGE follows a reason-then-predict paradigm, first generating an audio-grounded rationale to diagnose errors before predicting scores. The reinforcement learning stage uses a Thurstone-based preference objective that converts sampled score distributions into pairwise preference probabilities and rewards agreement with rankings derived from human judgments.

## [Imitation Policies Learn a Proprioceptive Anchor to Discount Noisy Object Positions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17658) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper shows that imitation policies for robot manipulation learn a proprioceptive anchor that discounts noisy object position readings by a variance-ratio weighting α = V/(V + s²), where V is the anchor's error variance and s² is the reading noise. The measured V predicted ten held-out sensitivities across two robomimic tasks, two learners, and four noise levels with mean absolute error 0.025, far better than classical errors-in-variables regression (0.37). This work provides a theoretical explanation for how imitation policies handle noisy observations and offers a predictive law that could help researchers design more robust robot learning systems. It also reveals a gap between training-time discounting and closed-loop deployment sensitivity, which is important for real-world robotics applications. The discount does not carry over to deployment: closed-loop sensitivity to a persistent reading offset exceeded α in all 15 policies that passed the registered gates, though the stricter prediction that all would follow the offset fully held for 8. Lower α was associated with lower success, steeply for diffusion policies, and a registered prospective test confirmed that measured α predicted success before any rollout within 0.03 (95% intervals ±0.08), although training noise predicted it equally well. All results are in simulation with synthetic Gaussian noise, and every test was registered before its data.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Imitation learning trains robot policies by mimicking demonstrations, but demonstrations often contain noisy object position readings. Classical errors-in-variables (EIV) models account for measurement error in regression but assume the model uses only the noisy reading, whereas here the policy also leverages the robot's own state (proprioception) as an anchor. Robomimic is a standard benchmark for evaluating imitation learning and offline reinforcement learning on robotic manipulation tasks.

A new method fine-tunes a supervisory language model to reconcile logical inconsistencies in LLM-based forecasts while improving accuracy, achieving a 9.3% better Brier score and 82-90% reduction in structural inconsistency on ForecastBench. Logical inconsistency in LLM forecasts undermines their reliability for decision-makers, and this work shows that consistency and accuracy need not be traded off, which could lead to more trustworthy AI forecasting systems. The method leverages the reasoning behind each forecast to jointly reconcile inconsistencies and improve accuracy, and it outperforms naive approaches like projection onto consistent forecasts, with ablations showing no alternative matches its simultaneous gains.

## [Superfluid: Open-Source Local LLM Server for Distributed Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17753) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Superfluid is a new open-source LLM server that unifies multiple inference runtimes (llama.cpp, MLX, BaseRT) under a single scheduler for efficient local inference on single devices and across heterogeneous machine fleets. It achieves significant performance gains, such as reducing first-token latency from 291-612 seconds to 1.5 seconds on an Apple M5 Pro when eight agents are active. This addresses key challenges in local LLM deployment, such as resource contention on single devices and underutilization of heterogeneous hardware, making it easier for developers and small teams to serve models privately and cost-effectively. The system's ability to scale across machines and handle failures without losing requests could democratize access to high-throughput LLM inference. Superfluid writes every token to a durable log before sending to the client, ensuring reliability, and it dynamically distributes work based on measured machine speed. On twelve Mac Studio M3 Ultras, it sustained 10,656 tokens per second across 38,563 requests from 384 agents without failure, and it is available under the Apache-2.0 license.

**Background**: Local LLM inference involves running models on personal devices like laptops or desktops, which offers privacy, no per-token costs, and offline operation. However, serving these models efficiently is challenging due to limited resources and the need to handle multiple concurrent requests from agents and users. Superfluid builds on existing runtimes like llama.cpp (a popular open-source library for LLM inference) and MLX (Apple's array framework for machine learning) to create a unified solution.
