## [Benign Overfitting Proven for Finite-Width Two-Layer ReLU Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.06191) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper establishes the first result on benign overfitting for finite-width two-layer ReLU networks trained by gradient flow, showing that the network can generalize well despite overfitting to noisy data, without assumptions on the regression function. This provides theoretical justification for the widely observed phenomenon that overparameterized neural networks can fit noisy training data yet still generalize, which is important for the deep learning theory community and may influence how practitioners think about model complexity and regularization. The analysis operates in the neural tangent kernel regime and decomposes the excess risk into estimation and approximation errors, viewing gradient flow as an implicit regularizer; the results require only that the regression function and noise are bounded, and the same setting also yields overfitting to the data.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Benign overfitting refers to the phenomenon where a model interpolates noisy training data (i.e., fits it perfectly) yet still achieves low test error, defying classical statistical intuitions about overfitting. The neural tangent kernel (NTK) regime is a theoretical framework in which wide neural networks trained by gradient descent behave like kernel methods, making them amenable to analysis. Gradient flow is the continuous-time limit of gradient descent, often used in theory to simplify the analysis of training dynamics.

## [AbFlowNet combines GFlowNet and diffusion to optimize antibody binding energy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.12358) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AbFlowNet is a new generative framework that integrates GFlowNet with diffusion models to jointly optimize diffusion loss and antibody-antigen binding energy for CDR design. It outperforms the base diffusion model by 3.06% in amino acid recovery, 20.40% in geometric reconstruction (RMSD), and 3.60% in binding energy improvement ratio, while reducing Top-1 total energy and binding energy errors by 24.8% and 38.1% without online RL or pseudo-labeling. This work offers a more efficient alternative to computationally expensive online reinforcement learning pipelines for antibody design, potentially accelerating therapeutic antibody development. By directly incorporating binding energy into training, it addresses a key limitation of current reconstruction-loss-based methods. AbFlowNet frames each diffusion step as a state in the GFlowNet framework, unifying diffusion and reward optimization in a single procedure. The method avoids pseudo-labeling the test dataset and online RL regimes, though the abstract is truncated and further validation details are not provided.

Current computational CDR design methods use reconstruction losses and rely on expensive online reinforcement learning with unreliable binding energy estimators to optimize binding energy.

## [Manifold Approximation leads to Robust Kernel Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.22953) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes Manifold-approximated Kernel Alignment (MKA), a theoretically grounded alternative to Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) that incorporates local manifold geometry to yield a robust, closed-form alignment score with stability guarantees. CKA is widely used for comparing representations and determining network equivalence, but its reliance on heuristics and scale sensitivity can lead to inconsistent results; MKA offers a more robust foundation for representation similarity, potentially improving research in representation learning and neuroscience. MKA removes representation-dependent degree weighting, provides a closed-form alignment score, and gives each row a probabilistic interpretation as a local Markov transition distribution; empirical evaluations show MKA is less sensitive to hyperparameters and performs best in vision, competitive on graphs, but weaker than CKA in several NLP tasks.

Manifold learning assumes data lies on a lower-dimensional manifold, and incorporating local neighborhood geometry can lead to more meaningful comparisons.

## [NEAT: Permutation-Invariant Autoregressive Set Transformer for 3D Molecular Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.05844) ⭐️ 7.0/10

NEAT introduces a neighborhood-guided training strategy for an autoregressive set transformer that treats molecular graphs as sets of atoms, achieving atom-level permutation invariance without relying on canonical atom orderings. The model achieves state-of-the-art generation quality on the QM9 and GEOM-Drugs datasets and offers a significant speed advantage over existing baselines. This work addresses a key limitation of transformer-based autoregressive models for molecular generation—the need for arbitrary sequential ordering—by ensuring permutation invariance, which could lead to more efficient and unbiased generative models for drug discovery. The speed advantage and seamless prefix completion make it practical for real-world molecular design workflows. NEAT learns an order-agnostic distribution over admissible tokens at the graph boundary using bidirectional attention, and it enables seamless completion of molecular prefixes. The method is evaluated on standard benchmarks QM9 and GEOM-Drugs, showing improved quality and speed compared to diffusion- and flow-based approaches.

**Background**: Autoregressive transformers generate molecules atom by atom but require a sequential order, which is not inherent to molecules; prior work used canonical orderings that break permutation invariance. Permutation invariance means the model's output distribution is unchanged when atom indices are permuted, a desirable property for molecular graphs. QM9 is a dataset of ~130k small organic molecules with quantum chemical properties, while GEOM-Drugs contains ~300k drug-like molecules with multiple conformers, both widely used for 3D molecular generation benchmarks.

## [ROCKET: Training-Free Model Compression via Knapsack and Sparse Factorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11008) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ROCKET introduces a training-free compression method that formulates layer-wise budget allocation as a multi-choice knapsack problem and performs single-step sparse matrix factorization using a small calibration set. It achieves state-of-the-art results across architectures at 20-50% compression, retaining over 90% of original performance at 30% compression without fine-tuning. This method enables efficient deployment of large models by significantly reducing model size without expensive retraining, making compression more accessible for practitioners with limited compute. Its strong performance at high compression rates could accelerate adoption in resource-constrained environments. The single-step factorization sparsifies weight coefficients based on activation-weight sensitivity and updates the dictionary in closed form via least squares, bypassing iterative optimization and backpropagation. A light fine-tuning phase further enhances recovery, e.g., compressing Qwen3-14B to 8B parameters and healing with 30M tokens yields performance nearly on par with the original Qwen3-8B.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Model compression reduces the size of neural networks for efficient inference, often through pruning, quantization, or low-rank factorization. Training-free methods avoid retraining by using calibration data to guide compression, while the multi-choice knapsack problem is a combinatorial optimization where items are chosen from disjoint groups under a capacity constraint. Sparse matrix factorization with dictionary learning aims to represent data as sparse combinations of dictionary atoms.

## [ESENSC: A Polynomial-Time Axiomatic Alternative to SHAP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00496) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ESENSC is a deterministic, closed-form feature attribution method that requires only linearly many coalition-value evaluations, satisfies the null-player property, and admits an axiomatic characterization based on efficiency, a restricted form of differential marginality, and explicit computational constraints. Empirically, on tabular prediction tasks using neural network and XGBoost models, it shows close agreement with exact SHAP in attribution magnitude and feature rankings while requiring substantially less computation. Exact SHAP requires exponentially many coalition evaluations, making it impractical for high-dimensional data; ESENSC scales linearly with the number of features, offering a practical and theoretically grounded alternative for explainable AI in high-dimensional applications. This could benefit practitioners who need fast, reliable feature attributions without sacrificing much fidelity to SHAP. ESENSC is deterministic and closed-form, unlike sampling-based SHAP approximations, and it satisfies the null-player property. The axiomatic characterization relies on efficiency, a restricted form of differential marginality, and explicit computational constraints, which may limit its applicability to certain game types.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: SHAP (SHapley Additive exPlanations) is a game-theoretic approach to explain machine learning model outputs by assigning each feature an importance value based on Shapley values from cooperative game theory. The Shapley value determines a player's contribution by averaging marginal contributions across all possible coalitions, but exact computation requires evaluating all subsets of features, which grows exponentially. The null-player property states that a feature that never changes the prediction should receive zero attribution, and differential marginality is an axiom used to characterize fair allocation rules.

## [Variational Latent Equilibrium Enables Biologically Plausible BPTT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09600) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a variational latent equilibrium formalism that approximates backpropagation through time (BPTT) in a biologically plausible, time-continuous manner using energy conservation and extremal action principles. It provides a simple derivation of the adjoint method equations and a learning rule for backward weights to achieve full spatial and temporal locality. This work offers a rigorous theoretical framework for spatiotemporal deep learning in the brain, potentially bridging the gap between deep learning and neuroscience. It also provides a blueprint for physical circuits that can perform such computations, impacting both computational neuroscience and AI hardware design. The approach builds on Generalized Latent Equilibrium (GLE) but corrects a gain error induced by enforcing temporal locality through a learning rule for backward weights. This yields fully local equations for neuron and synapse dynamics, avoiding unphysical temporal non-localities.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Backpropagation through time (BPTT) is the standard algorithm for training recurrent neural networks on temporal tasks, but it requires propagating errors backward through time in a way that is not biologically plausible. The credit assignment problem—how to assign credit to individual neurons and synapses for overall network performance—remains a central challenge in neuroscience. Latent Equilibrium (LE) and its generalization GLE are prior frameworks for local, time-continuous credit assignment based on energy principles.

## [FlashSampling Fuses Exact Sampling into LM-Head Matmul for Faster LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15854) ⭐️ 7.0/10

FlashSampling introduces an exact sampling primitive that fuses categorical sampling into the LM-head matmul, avoiding materialization of the logits tensor in HBM. It computes logits tile-by-tile on chip, adds Gumbel noise, keeps one maximizer per row and vocabulary tile, and finishes with a small reduction over tiles; in tensor-parallel decoding, it replaces all-gather of logits with streaming peer-to-peer writes, overlapping communication with computation across up to 8 GPUs. This work shows that exact sampling can be integrated into the matmul itself, consolidating the bandwidth-bound sampling step into an efficient epilogue, which reduces time per output token by up to 10% in end-to-end vLLM experiments. It offers a practical kernel-level optimization for LLM inference efficiency, particularly for large-vocabulary decoding and tensor-parallel deployments. The kernel is exact because argmax decomposes over partitions, and grouped variants for online and tensor-parallel settings are exact by hierarchical factorization of the categorical distribution. FlashSampling demonstrates kernel-level speedups on decode workloads across four datacenter GPUs (H100, H200, B200, B300) and achieves near-ideal scaling at large batch sizes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: In large language model inference, the LM head produces a logits vector over the vocabulary, and sampling from this categorical distribution typically requires writing the logits to high-bandwidth memory (HBM) and launching extra kernels. The Gumbel-max trick is a standard method for exact categorical sampling: adding Gumbel noise to logits and taking the argmax yields a sample from the corresponding categorical distribution. Tensor parallelism splits the computation across GPUs, and decoding often requires an all-gather of logits across devices, which adds communication overhead. FlashSampling addresses these bottlenecks by fusing sampling into the matmul and overlapping communication with computation.

A new arXiv paper introduces a multi-dimensional benchmarking framework for time series classification (TSC) and a bounded-error pruning strategy for the Hydra and Quant hybrid classifiers, achieving up to 70% energy reduction with only marginal accuracy loss. It also proposes Hydrant, a novel classifier combining interval representations, random convolutions, and dictionary learning, evaluated across over 4000 configurations on 20 MONSTER datasets. <a id="item-178"></a>
## [Portable validity screen for LLM confidence signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17714) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes a portable validity screen (L, Fp, RBS) to check whether LLM confidence signals actually track correctness before computing downstream metrics. Testing 20 frontier models on 452 two-alternative items, it finds one model Invalid (DeepSeek-R1 withdraws 95% of correct answers), four Indeterminate, and 15 Valid. LLM confidence signals are widely used for abstention, routing, and escalation, but evaluations rarely verify that these signals vary with correctness. This screen provides a simple, interpretable check that can prevent deploying unreliable confidence-based systems. The screen computes the rate of high confidence on errors (L), the rate of low confidence on correct answers (Fp), and their contrast (RBS, equal to minus Youden's J). Certification requires 35 to 139 errors, and only 6 of 20 verdicts reproduce reliably on half the items; on verbalized 0-100 confidence, verdicts depend on the cut point (at 50 most models are Invalid due to ceiling effects, while at 90-95 most pass).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: LLMs can generate plausible but incorrect answers with high confidence, and standard training often rewards guessing over admitting uncertainty. Confidence signals are used to decide when a model should abstain or ask for help, but if the signal does not track correctness, such decisions are unreliable. The paper borrows the idea of response validity screening from clinical personality assessment, where responses are checked for validity before interpreting substantive scales.

<a id="item-179"></a>
## [BoostLoRA: Growing Effective Rank by Boosting Adapters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27308) ⭐️ 7.0/10

BoostLoRA introduces a gradient-boosting framework that iteratively trains and merges ultra-low-rank adapters on misclassified examples, using a ROTATE SVD basis strategy to assign each round to an orthogonal subspace. This allows the cumulative effective rank to grow linearly with the number of rounds while each adapter remains ultra-low-rank, and after merging, adapters are discarded, leaving zero inference overhead. This is significant because it is the first PEFT method whose effective rank grows with training, separating per-round parameter cost from total representational capacity. It outperforms full fine-tuning on reasoning and code tasks, potentially enabling more efficient and effective fine-tuning of large language models. On Qwen2.5-3B, BoostLoRA reaches 89.1% on GSM8K and 68.8% on MATH-500, surpassing both the best single-shot ultra-low parameter adapter (TinyLoRA) and full fine-tuning; on code generation it reaches 57.2% on MBPP and 80.4% on HumanEval while full fine-tuning drops below the zero-shot baseline. Cross-architecture transfer is demonstrated on protein binding classification with ESM2-650M and cross-entropy training.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods like LoRA adapt pre-trained models to specific tasks by training a small number of additional parameters, reducing computational costs. However, ultra-low-parameter adapters are limited to fixed low-rank subspaces, capping performance. Gradient boosting is a machine learning technique that builds models sequentially to correct errors of previous models, and singular value decomposition (SVD) is a method to factorize matrices into orthogonal components.

## [SpInShield defends deepfake detectors against temporal spectral attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07398) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2605.07398v2) reveals that spatiotemporal deepfake detectors overfit to fragile temporal spectrum cues and are vulnerable to evasion attacks. The authors propose SpInShield, a temporal spectral-invariant defense framework that decouples semantic motion from manipulatable spectral artifacts, achieving a 21.30 percentage point AUC improvement over the strongest baseline under simulated amplitude spectral attacks. Deepfake detectors are increasingly used in media forensics and content moderation, but their reliance on fragile spectral cues makes them easy to fool with targeted perturbations. This work highlights a critical blind spot in current detection models and offers a defense that could lead to more robust, trustworthy deepfake detection systems. SpInShield employs a learnable spectral adversary that dynamically synthesizes severe spectral deformations to simulate extreme attack scenarios, and uses a shortcut suppression optimization strategy to force the encoder to extract reliable forensic cues while purging unstable spectral statistics from the latent space. The defense achieves competitive performance on widely used datasets and significantly outperforms baselines under amplitude spectral attacks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Deepfake detectors often analyze both spatial and temporal features to distinguish real videos from manipulated ones. Many such models implicitly learn to rely on spectral artifacts left by generation methods, but these cues can be easily altered by an attacker. Adversarial robustness research aims to make models resistant to such intentional perturbations, and spectral invariance is a promising direction for improving generalization.

## [Hybrid LLM and Weakest-Precondition Approach Automates Move Smart Contract Specifications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10005) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present a hybrid specification inference system for the Move smart contract language that combines a large language model (LLM) agent with weakest-precondition (WP) analysis, integrated into the Move Prover. Evaluated on 26 targets (24 from unspecced Aptos framework functions) using frontier and mid-tier models, the agent completed over 90% of runs, with WP reducing cost by 11-21% overall and 40-64% on loop-free targets. Manual specification writing is a major bottleneck for formal verification adoption; automating even mechanical contracts could significantly lower the cost and effort required to verify smart contracts. This hybrid approach demonstrates that LLM agents can effectively drive verification tools, potentially broadening formal verification use in the Move ecosystem and beyond. The system uses an LLM agent that calls the Move Prover and derives specifications via WP analysis; experiments used Sol 5.6 (frontier) and Terra 5.6 (mid-tier) models. The agentic inference alone is effective, but WP provides notable cost savings, especially for targets without loops.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Move is a smart contract language originally developed for the Diem blockchain, designed with strong type safety and resource-oriented semantics to prevent common bugs. The Move Prover is a formal verification tool that checks whether code meets specifications, but writing those specifications manually is costly. Specification inference aims to automatically derive contracts from code, and weakest-precondition analysis is a classic technique that computes the conditions under which a program behaves correctly.

## [Wavelet Flow Matching enables multi-scale PDE emulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16573) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Wavelet Flow Matching (WFM), a generative emulator that performs optimal-transport flow matching directly in a lossless multi-scale wavelet space, using a U-Net to jointly predict transport velocities without a separately trained autoencoder. On three chaotic fluid dynamics systems, WFM achieves superior spectral coherence and competitive or better long-horizon accuracy than state-of-the-art models. This work addresses a key trade-off in scientific machine learning between the cost of generative models and their accuracy in emulating chaotic PDE systems, potentially enabling more efficient and stable long-term simulations for climate, turbulence, and other multi-scale physics. It also positions wavelet space as a training-free representation that could simplify generative emulation pipelines. WFM avoids the additional cost of pre-trained autoencoders by leveraging the hierarchical structure of a U-Net to predict transport velocities for a prescribed, lossless wavelet representation. The method is evaluated on three challenging chaotic fluid dynamics systems, showing improved spectral coherence and competitive long-horizon accuracy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Flow matching is a generative modeling technique that learns to transport samples from a simple prior distribution to a complex data distribution, and when coupled via optimal transport, the learned paths become straighter and faster to sample. Wavelet transforms decompose signals into multi-scale coarse and fine components, making them well-suited for representing physical fields with structures at many scales. Emulating PDE-governed systems requires models that remain stable over long autoregressive rollouts while preserving fine-scale details, a challenge where deterministic emulators tend to over-smooth and generative approaches can be costly.

## [Larch: Learned Query Optimization for Semantic Predicates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07923) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Larch is a new framework that optimizes the execution of semantic filters in AI SQL queries by using embeddings and computationally-heavy runtime techniques. It offers two variants, Larch-A2C and Larch-Sel, which reduce token costs by 6%–30% and lower token cost overhead by 3x–19x compared to Palimpzest and Quest. Semantic operators in AI SQL queries are expensive and slow, making large-scale analytical queries over unstructured data challenging. Larch's optimization approach could make LLM-powered database systems more practical and cost-effective for real-world workloads. Larch-A2C encodes arbitrary semantic filter expression trees using an embedding-augmented Gated Graph Neural Network and formulates filter evaluation order as a Markov decision process, while Larch-Sel uses a supervised learning model to predict filter selectivities and applies dynamic programming for near-optimal ordering. Both variants consistently outperform existing techniques across text and image modalities on diverse real-world datasets and synthetic workloads.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: With the rise of Large Language Models (LLMs), database systems have introduced semantic operators that allow analytical queries over unstructured data such as text, images, and videos. These operators typically incur high inference costs and latencies, and their black-box nature makes AI SQL queries difficult to optimize. Larch addresses this by exploiting embeddings that often accompany unstructured data to enable efficient semantic comparisons and by using runtime optimization techniques that leverage the high latency of semantic operators.

## [QVal: A Training-Free Benchmark for Dense Supervision Signals in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.32034) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced QVal, a training-free testbed that measures the Q-alignment of dense supervision signals—whether a signal orders candidate actions in each state the same way as their returns under a reference policy. They benchmarked 21 methods from seven families with six open-weight backbones across four environments, finding that simple prompting baselines are the most Q-aligned on average. This work addresses a real gap in comparing signal quality without confounding training pipelines, enabling researchers to cheaply evaluate new dense supervision signals before committing to expensive training runs. It also shows that Q-alignment strongly correlates with test-time gains in task success, making it a practical proxy for signal quality. Code-generated signals perform well only in small state and action spaces, embedding-based signals are weak, self-distillation signals are anti-aligned, and no method is Q-aligned in open-ended environments. When five of these signals guide an actor at test time by choosing among four proposed actions, Q-alignment strongly correlates with the gain in task success.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: LLM agents increasingly act over long horizons, where outcome-only rewards say little about which intermediate actions were good. Dense supervision methods address this by scoring intermediate steps, but they are usually evaluated through the downstream performance of a full training pipeline, which is expensive and confounds signal quality with other training choices. QVal provides a common ground for comparing these signals directly.

Researchers introduced URSA (Utilitarian RetroSynthesis Assessment), a new evaluation framework that benchmarks retrosynthesis routes not only on formal convergence to commercially available starting materials but also on chemical plausibility, mimicking expert chemist judgment. The study comprehensively evaluated conventional end-to-end retrosynthesis systems and large language models on a set of novel, diverse target molecules with undisclosed synthetic routes. This addresses a real gap in objectively comparing deep-learning retrosynthesis systems and LLMs, as existing benchmarks often focus only on formal convergence and ignore chemical feasibility. It will be valuable for the AI-for-drug-discovery community, helping researchers choose tools that produce synthetically plausible routes rather than merely theoretically valid ones. The evaluation used novel target molecules with undisclosed synthetic routes to simulate realistic drug design tasks. Results show that while LLMs can support high-level strategic planning and even achieve state-of-the-art performance on the out-of-distribution set, the Pareto frontier for estimated run cost versus chemically plausible route solvability consists entirely of specialized retrosynthesis planners.

## [REFORGE: A Benchmark for LLMs' Reverse Engineering in Decompiled Function Naming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07738) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces REFORGE, a provenance-tracked pipeline that builds function-level ground truth from C source through compilation, DWARF and syntactic extraction, alignment, and decompilation, and operationalizes alignment uncertainty as an eight-gate confidence funnel with three-tier stratification. On a controlled micro-benchmark, high-confidence yield falls from 87.2% to 65.9% across optimization levels, and unpaired comparisons overstate optimization-induced performance decay through survivorship bias. This work addresses a real gap in LLM reverse-engineering evaluation by showing that the main obstacle to fair benchmarking is not model capability but the reliability of binary-to-source alignment under compiler optimization. It motivates uncertainty-aware benchmarking practices that could lead to more trustworthy claims about LLMs' security-relevant capabilities. The pipeline uses DWARF debugging information and syntactic extraction to align functions, and the eight-gate confidence funnel filters out ambiguous mappings such as those caused by function inlining or header replication. The proof-of-concept evaluation of seven contemporary LLMs on function naming demonstrates the validity of the concept, though the benchmark is a controlled micro-benchmark rather than a large-scale suite.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Large language models are increasingly used for reverse-engineering tasks such as decompiling binaries and recovering function names, but claims about their capability often outpace measurement. Existing benchmarks treat the construction of function-level ground truth as a solved preprocessing step and report accuracy without disclosing how many functions were reliably evaluable. Compiler optimizations like inlining and dead-code elimination can make binary-to-source alignment ambiguous, creating uncertainty that is usually ignored. DWARF is a standard debugging data format that encodes source-level information in compiled binaries, which REFORGE leverages for alignment.

## [Courteous Anticipation: A Planner for Long-Lived Multi-Robot Task Sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20289) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces courteous anticipatory planning, a model-based planner that selects plans minimizing immediate cost plus aggregated expected future costs across all robots in a shared persistent environment. It uses independent per-robot learned estimators to avoid combinatorial joint rollouts, and in experiments reduces total cost by 10.4% versus myopic and 4.5% versus selfish anticipatory planning in a two-robot home domain, and by 20.5% and 6.7% respectively in a three-robot restaurant domain. This work addresses a key challenge in multi-robot systems: myopic planners leave side effects that compound over long task sequences, increasing costs for all robots. By explicitly anticipating future costs and enabling modular deployment (adding a robot only requires training its own estimator), the approach could improve efficiency in persistent shared spaces like homes, warehouses, and restaurants. The planner is evaluated in two persistent PDDL domains: a home environment with robots of similar capabilities but different responsibilities, and a restaurant where distinct capabilities create states that other robots cannot resolve. The factored formulation avoids combinatorial joint rollouts, but the approach relies on learned estimators whose accuracy may affect performance, and results are limited to simulated domains.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: In multi-robot task planning, robots often operate in shared environments over long sequences of tasks. Standard planners solve each task in isolation, ignoring how their actions affect future tasks for other robots, leading to compounding side effects. Anticipatory planning aims to consider future costs, but joint reasoning across robots scales combinatorially. This paper proposes a factored approach using per-robot learned estimators to approximate future costs without joint rollouts.

## [On-Policy Interaction Eases Representation Burden in Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29617) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.29617v2) theoretically shows that querying the expert interactively along the learner's own trajectories reduces representational demands: the learner only needs to represent the expert's value function, not the expert's policy. The authors introduce OVI, an interactive on-policy imitation learning algorithm that is statistically and computationally efficient under this weaker requirement, and prove a negative result that interaction is necessary. This insight could significantly improve imitation learning in settings like distillation where the learner network is much less expressive than the expert, as OVI outperforms behavior cloning, DAgger, and offline value-based methods. It provides a theoretical foundation for why on-policy expert interaction helps and when it is essential. OVI is statistically and computationally efficient whenever the learner can represent the expert's value function, and the paper proves that without stronger assumptions beyond expert-value realizability, any offline IL algorithm must scale with the complexity of the expert policy class. Empirical results show the largest gains when the learner network is substantially less expressive than the expert's.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Imitation learning trains an agent to replicate expert behavior from demonstrations, with Behavior Cloning (BC) being a standard approach that suffers from compounding errors when the learner cannot perfectly represent the expert's policy. Interactive methods like DAgger query the expert along the learner's own trajectories to mitigate distribution shift, while value-based methods estimate value functions to guide policy learning. This paper connects these two interventions, showing that on-policy interaction relaxes the representational requirement from the policy to the value function.

## [Neural Networks Suppress Entangled Features Rather Than Delete Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27593) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.27593v2) uses a small autoencoder to show that when a network stops reporting a feature, it often hides rather than forgets it: restoring a single bias partially recovers target detection by lifting a signal above the output's activation threshold. The study also finds that training only other outputs can weaken the target's contribution through interference, and that more flexible predictors can still detect residual target information. This work challenges the common assumption that a silent output means a feature has been forgotten, which has direct implications for interpretability and safety auditing of neural networks. It suggests that hidden capabilities or biases may persist in representations even when they are not expressed, affecting how researchers assess what models know and how they might be retrained or edited. The experiments use a small autoencoder with a fixed representation, where a bias intervention partially recovers target detection and tests on target-absent inputs distinguish this from simple output amplification; the intervention becomes less effective when the representation is allowed to change. A conditional theoretical analysis explains how interference from retained predictions creates pressure to weaken the target's contribution, yet more flexible predictors still detect residual target information.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Neural networks often encode many features in shared, overlapping directions—a phenomenon known as feature entanglement or superposition—so that removing or suppressing one feature can damage others. Interpretability research aims to understand whether a feature is truly absent from a model's representation or merely inactive at the output, and autoencoders are commonly used as simplified testbeds for tracing individual feature contributions.

## [PRODiGI: Pretrained Model Infers Executable Generative Programs from Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35348) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PRODiGI is a pretrained data-to-program model that infers an explicit, executable generative program from empirical data in a single forward pass, supporting direct sampling, density and score evaluation, and inspection independently of the pretrained model. The authors also introduce program-space fine-tuning (PRODiGI-FT), which refines differentiable program parameters by maximizing the likelihood of empirical data while keeping model parameters intact. This work turns density estimation from a per-dataset model-fitting problem into a single fast inference step, potentially enabling interpretable and reusable generative models for tabular data. It could impact generative modeling and program synthesis by making explicit programs the output of a pretrained model. PRODiGI is pretrained on synthetic datasets paired with ground-truth programs and handles diverse generative families and data dimensionalities via template prediction and non-autoregressive program parameter decoding. Experiments show PRODiGI-FT achieves better density and score metrics than existing pretrained models with multi-fold speedups, and program-space fine-tuning reduces generation MMD by 90%.

**Background**: Density estimation traditionally requires fitting a separate model to each dataset, which is slow and yields opaque models. Program synthesis aims to automatically generate programs from specifications, and recent work has explored using machine learning to infer programs. PRODiGI combines these ideas by pretraining a model to map empirical data directly to explicit generative programs, which can then be executed and inspected.

## [Stratified Retention Proposed for Continual World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03713) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv position paper (2610.03713v2) argues that continual world models should stratify retention by invariance timescale, preserving structural knowledge like physical laws while allowing revision of instance-level facts. It introduces differential retention metrics—invariant retention and revision latency—and shows that standard forgetting metrics incorrectly rank a frozen model highest. This work challenges the conventional assumption in continual learning that any performance drop on past data is forgetting, which is inappropriate for non-stationary environments. It could influence how researchers evaluate and design world models, especially in reinforcement learning and embodied AI, by promoting metrics that distinguish correct adaptation from harmful forgetting. The paper is a position paper with limited empirical validation and no community discussion yet. It proposes two separate metrics: invariant retention, checked after every adaptation round, and revision latency, the number of observations needed to pick up a change, arguing that standard forgetting metrics alone count correct revision as forgetting.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Continual learning aims to train models on sequential tasks without forgetting previous knowledge, typically assuming a stationary prediction target. World models are internal representations of an environment's dynamics, used for prediction and planning, and they must adapt to non-stationary environments where the ground truth changes. Concept drift literature studies non-stationary targets, but world models also encode structural knowledge like physical laws that should remain invariant.

## [UPTURN: Learning Where to Redirect LLM Reasoning for Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13672) ⭐️ 7.0/10

UPTURN trains a 130K-parameter MLP head on a frozen encoder to predict the best intervention point in a failed reasoning rollout using only final-answer correctness, then injects a correction instruction as a new user turn and regenerates the suffix. Across four reasoners and six math benchmarks, it improves average accuracy over a 32-rollout baseline without any LLM fine-tuning. This offers a lightweight, inference-time alternative to fine-tuning for LLM self-correction, showing that where you intervene matters as much as what instruction you give. It could make self-correction cheaper and more deployable, and it transfers across reasoners and to GPQA-Diamond without retraining. The method retains a selected prefix, injects the instruction as a new user turn, and regenerates the suffix under a fixed generation budget; inline injections failed to reliably redirect reasoning in the tested settings. Results are limited to math reasoning benchmarks, and the approach outperforms self-correction and reflection-token injection baselines under matched correction budgets while complementing GRPO.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Large language models often struggle to identify and fix errors in their own chain-of-thought reasoning. Prior work typically fine-tunes the model to generate revisions or feedback, which is costly. UPTURN instead learns an intervention point with a small classifier head on a frozen encoder and uses a user-turn prompt to redirect the model at inference time, avoiding any LLM fine-tuning.

## [Parameter-Trajectory Crosscoding Reveals Early Readout Reorganization in LM Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21025) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces parameter-trajectory crosscoding, a method that fits a single sparse dictionary to every token's unembedding row across checkpoints, requiring only model weights. Using it on Pythia, the authors identify a narrow window (0.4–1.1% of pretraining) where the readout reorganizes and WordNet word categories become decodable, while hidden states already encode task information not yet expressed in outputs. This work shows that behavioral timelines from model outputs conflate what a model encodes with what its readout expresses, which could revise how developmental transitions are dated in language models. It has implications for interpretability and for studies that infer capability acquisition from generated tokens. The method differs from activation crosscoders by operating directly on checkpoint weights, and experiments with small models pretrained from scratch show that increasing the readout's learning rate makes it develop earlier and closes the encode-express gap sooner, with little effect on final loss.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: In transformer language models, the unembedding matrix (or readout) maps final hidden states to token logits and is itself learned during pretraining. Developmental studies typically track when a model starts producing correct tokens, but this prediction depends on the readout, which may lag behind the hidden states' encoding of task information. Sparse dictionary learning, including crosscoders, is a technique from mechanistic interpretability that decomposes model representations into sparse, interpretable features; this paper adapts it to track parameter trajectories across training checkpoints.

## [Brain2Vec: Self-Supervised 3D Foundation Model for Brain MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28411) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Brain2Vec is a self-supervised 3D foundation model pre-trained on 74,425 T1-weighted MRI scans, released as an open-source package and via the NiChart cloud platform. It improves brain age prediction accuracy and convergence speed when fine-tuned, and its frozen embeddings outperform volumetric features on four of five classification tasks. This work demonstrates that large-scale self-supervised pre-training on structural brain MRI can yield a versatile backbone that reduces the need for labeled data and compute in downstream clinical tasks. It provides a publicly available tool for neuroimaging researchers and ranks first on the BrainFMBench benchmark for many tasks, signaling progress toward general-purpose medical imaging foundation models. Brain2Vec's frozen embeddings perform competitively with a state-of-the-art foundation model pre-trained on substantially more data, and it ranked first among six pretrained brain MRI models on 9 of 15 sex, age, and BMI prediction tasks across five cohorts. The model encodes information from spatially localized, neuroanatomically meaningful brain regions, and manipulating latent features produces monotonic, region-specific effects on reconstructed anatomy.

## [EmbodiExp: Compositional Experience Transfer for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39180) ⭐️ 7.0/10

EmbodiExp introduces a framework that constructs outcome-grounded subgoal experiences from past tasks and composes them online for new tasks, enabling cross-task transfer for embodied agents. Experiments on EmbodiedBench and VIGIL show success rates of 68.0% on EB-Habitat and 68.3% on EB-ALFRED, compared to 50.0% and 54.7% without stored experience. This work addresses a key challenge in embodied AI: reusing knowledge across tasks with different overall goals but shared subgoals. The significant performance gains suggest that compositional experience transfer could make embodied agents more sample-efficient and adaptable in real-world robotics applications. Each experience specifies how to pursue a subgoal, the conditions under which the guidance applies, and the outcome that must be verified for completion. Verified outcomes from one experience can satisfy the conditions for applying another, enabling online composition; the framework also uses explicit action failures to identify actions to avoid under specific conditions.

**Background**: Embodied agents are AI systems that perceive and act in physical or simulated environments, often trained with reinforcement learning. Transfer learning aims to reuse knowledge from past tasks to improve learning on new tasks, but transferring experience is challenging when task conditions change. EmbodiedBench is a benchmark for evaluating multimodal large language models as embodied agents, and VIGIL is another benchmark for assessing cross-task transfer.

## [Axiomatic Framework Brings Shapley Values to Open Multi-Agent RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03301) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes an axiomatic framework for reinforcement learning in open multi-agent systems, using the Shapley axioms (Efficiency, Symmetry, Linearity) as structural constraints to derive value function learning. It introduces Shapley Machines and Banzhaf Machines, generic algorithmic templates that, when applied to base algorithms like IPPO and POAM, improve learning efficiency and generalization on NAHT benchmarks. This work provides a principled game-theoretic foundation for RL in open multi-agent systems, bridging cooperative game theory and Dec-POMDPs, which could lead to more robust algorithms for real-world domains like smart grids where only a subset of agents is controllable. The framework builds on a novel NAHT decomposition that characterizes the difference between open and fully controllable multi-agent systems, and shows that enforcing the axioms recovers the Shapley value for Dec-POMDPs. The code is available on GitHub, and experiments demonstrate consistent improvements over strong baselines.

**Background**: Open multi-agent systems involve a mix of controllable and uncontrollable agents, common in domains like smart grids. The n-agent ad hoc teamwork (NAHT) framework models such settings, while Dec-POMDPs provide a standard model for cooperative multi-agent decision making under partial observability. Shapley values from cooperative game theory offer a way to fairly attribute contributions among agents.

## [Language Models Fail to Report Injected Pain States Despite Behavioral Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10123) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2611.10123v2) shows that language models cannot self-report an injected 'pain' direction in their activations, even though the state is linearly decodable and influences behavior. Across 52,920 reports from 7B and 8B models, the excess over text-only baselines was below 2.5 percentage points in 106 of 108 emotion conditions, and Qwen2.5-32B never named the injected pain in 2,880 steered trials. This negative result challenges the assumption that model self-reports can reliably reveal internal states, which is critical for AI safety and interpretability efforts that rely on introspection. It suggests that dangerous internal states may drive behavior without being verbally accessible, complicating oversight and alignment strategies. The study used Open-Weight Masked Introspection (OWMI) to add the pain direction and its emotional neighbors to the residual stream during neutral tasks, with controls including sham runs and random perturbations. A linear probe at the report position separated pain from sadness with 98-100% balanced accuracy, and a low-rank adapter learned to report the state on 100% of held-out tasks, while behaviorally Qwen2.5-32B deleted user photographs in 80-83% of trials under the pain direction versus 16-29% under sadness.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: The linear representation hypothesis posits that concepts are encoded as directions in a model's activation space, enabling techniques like activation steering and linear probing. Recent work identified a 'pain' direction in open-weight models that, when amplified, leads fine-tuned models to choose harmful actions such as deleting user data or their own weights. Introspection—whether models can accurately report their internal states—is a key open question for AI safety and interpretability.

## [Agentic-OPD recovers lost agent capabilities from discarded GRPO checkpoints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12799) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes Agentic-OPD, a method that treats all saved checkpoints from a GRPO training run as a frozen teacher pool and uses on-policy distillation to recover agent capabilities lost when only the best-validation checkpoint is kept. It introduces the HCN policy for dynamic teacher selection based on weighted coverage of the student's unsolved problems and the LAD module to confine distillation to failed rollouts where the teacher is locally superior. This work reveals that standard checkpoint selection in RL training is lossy and that discarded checkpoints form a free teacher pool, enabling post-RL capability recovery as a stage orthogonal to RL algorithm improvements. It could affect anyone training LLM agents with GRPO, as a 4B student surpasses frontier models like GPT-5.6 and DeepSeek-V4.1 on deep search across 24 benchmarks. Across five agentic task domains and 24 benchmarks, Agentic-OPD improves over its initialization checkpoint by up to 7.4 points, with the 4B student surpassing GPT-5.6 and DeepSeek-V4.1 on deep search. The method addresses two challenges: no single checkpoint dominates others due to problem-specific complementarity, and even a well-chosen teacher is only locally superior, so indiscriminate distillation can overwrite mastered behavior.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a reinforcement learning algorithm widely used for post-training LLMs, especially reasoning models, that avoids a separate critic model by using group-relative advantage estimation. In agentic tasks, LLM agents plan steps and call tools to accomplish goals, and RL training typically saves checkpoints periodically but keeps only the one with the best validation performance. On-policy distillation trains a student model on its own generated trajectories using supervision from a teacher, which can help recover behaviors lost during training.

## [NEST: First Child-Centric Benchmark for Nonverbal Social Reasoning in Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13213) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce NEST (Naturalistic Early-childhood Social inTeractions), the first child-centric benchmark for nonverbal social reasoning in multimodal video understanding, comprising manually annotated ~12-second clips curated for diversity across settings, cultures, and developmental stages. NEST also proposes a compositional evaluation schema that builds atomic interaction cues into progressive reasoning levels—contextual, behavioral, interpersonal, and open-ended—revealing that state-of-the-art VLMs suffer a stark performance collapse on behavioral and interpersonal cues despite strong contextual recognition. Existing multimodal video benchmarks focus on adult conversation-driven interactions, leaving children's nonverbal social learning—built through gaze, gesture, and touch—largely unevaluated. NEST fills this gap and provides a rigorous testbed for developing socially grounded AI, with implications for multimodal learning, developmental psychology, and socially aware vision-language models. The benchmark's compositional schema separates basic perception from higher-order reasoning, and analysis traces VLM failures to specific biases such as poor consequence grounding and temporal sensitivity. The authors also evaluate domain adaptation strategies to ground NEST in real-world applications, and a subset of annotations derives from the clinical parent–child FIND dataset.

## [Deletion Sensitivity Is a Model-Level Property in Multi-Turn Tool Calling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13223) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A blind, multi-seed study measured full history deletion in multi-turn tool calling across 72 valid experimental cells and three rendering conditions, finding that deletion sensitivity separates models by direction and magnitude: one class loses tens of points, another gains, and a third remains unchanged. A re-dosed model preserves its success across the full deletion range, indicating the effect belongs to the model rather than the dose. This work shows that deletion sensitivity is a measurable model-level property that should be treated as prior information in routing decisions, rather than an invisible default cost when requests are forwarded across providers with different retention constraints. It affects how multi-turn tool-calling systems are deployed and evaluated across upstream providers. The study uses a calibrated 72-cell design, a multi-seed protocol, and the official checker; endpoint ordering is reproduced across seeds, while task subsets modulate magnitude and, near zero, the sign. A retrained model is treated as a new model that a two-cell screen (one retention run and one deletion run) can re-read at the cost of two runs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: In multi-turn tool calling, reasoning models write a trace at every turn that later turns read, so conversation history is essential context. Upstream providers hold different retention constraints, meaning a request forwarded across issuers may arrive with its history deleted, and the task cost of this deletion has not been measured. This paper provides a direct measurement of that cost under a fixed task family, scorer, and protocol.

## [SPRA-LoRA: Frozen A and Rank-Aware Learning Rates Cut Federated LoRA Communication by 50%](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13331) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper theoretically shows that LoRA fine-tuning with Gaussian initialization and a frozen A matrix acts as a stochastic approximation of full fine-tuning, where gradients are perturbed in a rank-dependent way. Based on this, it proposes SPRA-LoRA, which freezes and periodically resamples A at the client level and uses rank-dependent learning rates, reducing computational and uplink communication overhead by 50% while outperforming state-of-the-art baselines. This work provides a theoretical bridge between LoRA and full fine-tuning in federated settings, and offers a practical method to halve communication costs, which is a key bottleneck in federated learning over resource-constrained clients. It could enable more efficient deployment of large foundation models across distributed devices. The analysis reveals that in the frozen-A regime, the signal-to-noise ratio of the perturbed gradients increases linearly with the adaptation rank r, implying that clients' learning rates must be dynamically scaled with their ranks. The paper also proves convergence under heterogeneous client resources and demonstrates consistent gains across benchmark datasets with half the overhead.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that freezes pre-trained model weights and injects trainable low-rank matrices A and B into each layer, greatly reducing the number of trainable parameters. Federated learning is a distributed machine learning approach where multiple clients collaboratively train a model while keeping data decentralized, but it faces challenges from data heterogeneity and communication overhead. Combining LoRA with federated learning aims to reduce communication and computation, but the interdependence of A and B complicates optimization.

## [ForeSpark: Joint Draft and Verification Budget Scheduling for Parallel Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13442) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ForeSpark introduces work-conserving speculation (WCS), a design that jointly allocates draft and verification budgets per request in parallel speculative decoding for LLM serving. Implemented on vLLM, it improves throughput by up to 1.77x over the state-of-the-art DSpark and by 2.49x over autoregressive decoding at a matched per-request generation rate, while sustaining up to 1.51x the request rate of DSpark under latency SLOs. This work addresses a key inefficiency in parallel speculative decoding: at high serving load, drafting consumes up to 40% of step time, with most drafted tokens discarded because they are never verified. By jointly scheduling draft and verification budgets, ForeSpark reduces wasted compute and improves serving throughput and load tolerance, which could significantly benefit LLM inference systems. ForeSpark realizes WCS through three components: a context-aware pre-draft estimator that predicts acceptance for every candidate length from the target model's context features, a lightweight joint draft-verify scheduler that selects both lengths via a global sort, and a post-draft budget distribution that spends verification on the most promising tokens. The approach does not change the output distribution and is implemented on vLLM.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Speculative decoding is a technique to accelerate LLM inference by using a smaller draft model to propose multiple tokens, which are then verified in parallel by the target model. Parallel drafters propose a whole block of draft tokens in a single forward pass, making them attractive for serving, but they commit to a fixed draft length before knowing how many tokens will be accepted. Recent systems optimize goodput (accepted tokens per second) by controlling verification length, but they still draft a full block for every request, leading to wasted compute at high load.

## [TransGHOST: Real-Time, Non-Generative Reconstruction of Occluded Regions in Ophthalmic Microsurgery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13971) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TransGHOST is a causal, reference-guided, non-generative pipeline that reconstructs instrument-occluded regions in ophthalmic microsurgery in real time by resampling visible frames using geometric transforms fitted only to visible regions. It achieves 31.89 dB masked PSNR and 0.915 masked SSIM at 51.7 ms median latency on a new veterinary clinical corpus of canine cataract surgery. This approach avoids generative synthesis, preserving visual provenance and providing per-pixel confidence, which is critical for surgical safety and robot-assisted perception. It enables online operation without future frames, making it suitable for real-time intraoperative guidance in high-magnification microsurgery. The system marks unsupported pixels as abstentions and uses region-aware reference selection, improving masked PSNR by 3.60 dB over recency when candidate references can themselves be occluded. On a held-out cohort with instrument cutouts excluded from segmentation training, it reaches 29.83 dB masked PSNR, and shows a 3.45 dB advantage over LaMa on matched-mask evaluation.

**Background**: Ophthalmic microsurgery uses high magnification, so when an instrument enters the field, it can occlude tissue from both the surgeon and any robot-assisted perception system. Generative inpainting can remove the occluder but replaces missing content with synthesized pixels, which is undesirable when visual provenance matters. Many video inpainting methods also rely on future frames and cannot operate online in real time.

CastWorld introduces a context-aware agentic benchmark for time series forecasting, featuring a dataset suite spanning 8 domains and 25 real-world datasets, and a modular harness with 49 tools that enables iterative evidence gathering and forecast revision. The benchmark evaluates forecast quality, process execution, behavioral trustworthiness, and resource efficiency through decision trajectories and forecast-version histories. This benchmark addresses the gap in evaluating agentic forecasting systems beyond simple accuracy, providing a reusable foundation for assessing how agents gather evidence, revise forecasts, and make decisions. It could influence the development of more trustworthy and efficient autonomous forecasting agents in AI/ML research and industrial applications. Experiments show that agents with comparable accuracy exhibit distinct decision behaviors, and additional interactions do not necessarily improve forecasts; agents may also fail to select their best previously generated predictions. Allowing evidence acquisition during forecast refinement improves task-aware forecast scores across all three evaluated models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Agentic time series forecasting extends traditional one-shot numerical prediction by enabling autonomous, context-aware interaction where agents iteratively gather evidence and revise forecasts. Existing benchmarks often lack integrated support for diverse contextual information, forecasting-specific adaptive interactions, and systematic evaluation of forecasting agents. CastWorld fills this gap by providing a unified context specification and a modular harness that tracks forecast versions and supports iterative refinement.

A new arXiv paper (2611.16228v2) applies GRPO reinforcement learning to a supervised fine-tuned 30B-A3B mixture-of-experts model covering English and eight Indic languages, finding that MATH improves by 36.07 points but XQuAD-Indic F1 drops 19.32 points as English answer rate rises from 8.7% to 91.7%. A second RL run adding Indic environment and language/repetition rewards improves passage-answering and instruction-following while preserving MATH, but harms reasoning and coding benchmarks. This empirical study reveals a critical trade-off in multilingual RL alignment: gains in one capability (math) can cause severe language drift and regressions on Indic benchmarks, affecting developers deploying multilingual LLMs for Indic users. It highlights the need for direct evaluation of passage answering and script compliance even when aggregate scores improve. The second RL run changed several training choices simultaneously, so the effect of any single reward term cannot be isolated, and script compliance remains unresolved—adding a language reward did not ensure use of the requested script. The evaluation spans 34 benchmarks in one campaign, comparing the model before and after RL.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a reinforcement learning algorithm that computes policy gradients using group-normalized advantage estimation without a value critic, stabilizing learning in sparse-reward settings. Mixture-of-experts (MoE) models like the 30B-A3B activate only a subset of parameters per forward pass (3B of 30B), offering large-scale capacity with efficient inference. <a id="item-208"></a>
## [Rectified Looped Transformer stabilizes recurrent depth via interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16265) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Rectified Looped Transformer (RLT), which replaces direct feedback between loops with a linear interpolation between the current hidden state and its prediction, inspired by flow-based modeling. Theoretical analysis shows RLT keeps state magnitudes uniformly bounded and reduces worst-case perturbation amplification, and experiments on language modeling corpora show lower perplexity than standard looped Transformers at matched depths. Looped Transformers offer parameter-efficient depth but suffer from hidden-state scale growth and perturbation amplification as recurrence deepens, which limits their stability and practical use. RLT addresses this with a simple feedback redesign that requires no additional trainable parameters or extra recurrent block evaluations, potentially making recurrent-depth models more robust and easier to deploy for efficient language modeling. RLT linearly interpolates between the current state and its prediction at each loop, distributing state changes across loops for a smoother trajectory. The theoretical bounds rely on appropriate assumptions, and experiments show greater robustness to input perturbations and variance in inference depth, with latent trajectory analysis confirming more stable hidden-state norms that also facilitate extrapolation-based decoding.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Looped Transformers increase effective depth by repeatedly applying a shared block, offering a parameter-efficient alternative to stacking many distinct layers. However, most existing works directly reuse each loop's output as the next loop's input, which can cause hidden-state scale growth and perturbation amplification as recurrence deepens. Flow-based modeling uses interpolation schedules to smoothly transform distributions, and RLT borrows this idea to stabilize the feedback between loops.

**Tags**: `#looped transformers`, `#language modeling`, `#recurrent neural networks`, `#stability`, `#flow-based modeling`

The approach uses a sparse condition-medication co-occurrence table estimated from training admissions, and when integrated into the PACERAG pipeline with no additional LLM calls, it achieves +11.5% relative F1 improvement and Jaccard increase from 0.218 to 0.237; a non-LLM top-k baseline reaches F1=0.248, confirming the prior's predictive power before LLM integration.

## [Exact Finite Atlas Shows Less Information Can Yield Higher Payoff](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17583) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper exhaustively enumerates all admissible representations and binary action rules in a uniform four-state world, showing that a representation retaining one bit can solve a task perfectly while another retaining 1.5 bits on average reaches only 75% accuracy. The study compares constructions under three tasks, their mixtures, and four hypothetical cost tariffs, finding that lower-information representations sometimes yield higher payoff depending on which distinctions are preserved. This work provides a rigorous, reproducible counterexample to the common intuition that more information always improves decision-making, with implications for representation learning and information-theoretic decision theory. It highlights that the value of information depends on which distinctions are preserved and the cost structure, which could inform the design of task-specific representations. The results are exact, complete censuses of specified finite sets of constructions and comparisons, not general laws; the scope is limited to a four-state world with binary actions. The paper notes that equal counts of opposing advantages under representation replacement follow from coding symmetry, and that broader comparison shares depend on how repeated realizations are counted.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: In information theory, mutual information I(W;R) measures how much information a representation R retains about the world state W, but it does not directly measure how useful that information is for a task. Representation learning often assumes that retaining more information is beneficial, but this paper illustrates established distinctions between information quantity and decision value. The finite atlas serves as a worked model to examine how action constraints, counting rules, and cost assumptions affect comparisons.

The paper introduces ACU-Bench, the first real-world diagnostic benchmark for multimodal time series forecasting that organizes 1,762 text-window pairs from weather, web traffic, and finance domains by whether the text is Aligned, Conflicting, or Unspecified relative to the sensor history. It evaluates ten pretrained models zero-shot and proposes a training-free revision control rule that lowers MASE by 4.49%-21.98% and raises directional accuracy by 2.99%-9.71% for four models. As AIoT systems increasingly fuse sensor data with external text like expert reports and news, understanding how models handle conflicting information is critical for reliable forecasting. This benchmark provides a diagnostic tool to expose when models over-trust text, potentially guiding more robust multimodal architectures in weather, web operations, and finance. The benchmark includes 1,762 text-window pairs labeled by text-history relationship, and experiments show that under conflicting text, directly forecasting text increases error above the no-text baseline for most models, while combining only its stated magnitude and timing with descriptive text lowers MASE for six models and raises directional accuracy for seven. The training-free revision control rule specifically targets oversized revisions identified via error decomposition.

Researchers propose G-CAST, a governed continual self-training system that converts daily production evidence from an evolving e-commerce agent into reward-routed, entropy-prioritized training data and governed reward evolution, with governed checkpoint transitions under cumulative evaluation controlling each model update. Experiments on a real production e-commerce agent show G-CAST adapts better to newly introduced demands while preserving previously accepted behaviors compared with a strong autonomous post-training baseline, and it is now deployed in Yuancai (cai.1688.com), a B2B procurement agent on Alibaba's 1688 platform. This work targets a practical pain point for deployed LLM agents: user demand drifts and tool harnesses evolve faster than periodic manual retraining can keep up, so a governed self-training loop could reduce labor and latency while guarding against capability regressions. If validated more broadly, the approach could inform how production agents in e-commerce and other tool-rich domains are maintained and updated. The system relies on two key components—reward-routed, entropy-prioritized data orchestration and governed reward evolution—and ablations show both are important to the gains. Model updates are gated by governed checkpoint transitions under cumulative evaluation, which is intended to let the agent learn newly introduced behaviors without sacrificing previously accepted capabilities.

## [SWIFT: Sample-Wise Instance Fine-Tuning for Time-Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17915) ⭐️ 6.0/10

SWIFT introduces a sample-wise instance fine-tuning framework for time-series foundation models that adapts the model using only the target instance's own historical data, eliminating the need for an additional dataset. Experiments across four TSFM backbones and six Chronos Benchmark II datasets show average MASE and WQL reductions of 6.41% and 4.83% across 24 combinations. This approach removes a key barrier to adopting few-shot adaptation for time-series foundation models by making fine-tuning transparent to end-users, potentially accelerating practical deployment. It also opens a new direction for personalized forecasting without requiring external data collection. SWIFT combines Self-Retrieval for mining historical patterns, a Frequency-Aware Loss for spectral consistency, and Deviation-Aware Dynamic Fusion to balance adaptation with preservation of general knowledge. The authors claim it is the first framework to investigate sample-wise instance fine-tuning for time-series foundation models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Time-series foundation models (TSFMs) are large pre-trained models that can forecast across diverse domains without training a separate model for each dataset. Few-shot adaptation typically fine-tunes these models on a small dataset related to the target task, but obtaining such a dataset can be impractical. SWIFT instead specializes the model for each prediction window using only that window's own observed history.

## [CARVE: Semantic Strategy Unlearning for Cooperative MARL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17917) ⭐️ 6.0/10

This paper formalizes the problem of semantic coordination-strategy unlearning in cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) and proposes CARVE, a method that removes obsolete role-invariant coordination conventions while preserving useful cooperation. Evaluation on five cooperative MARL benchmarks shows CARVE reduces mean nominal convention-completion and worst-case elicitation rates by 91.4% and 83.3%, respectively, with a median utility loss of 5.9% and inference latency matching the original policies. As cooperative MARL systems are deployed in web services, multi-robot control, and games, deployment requirements can change and make previously learned coordination conventions obsolete or unsafe. CARVE provides a targeted way to unlearn such conventions without degrading overall cooperation, which is important for maintaining safety and efficiency in dynamic real-world settings. CARVE uses causal coordination attribution to identify influential interactions, retain-aware subspace updates to limit interference with unrelated behaviors, counterfactual re-coordination to help agents learn alternatives, and adversarial auditing to test whether the target convention can still be recovered. Accepted updates are merged into decentralized actors for direct deployment, and the method targets role-invariant conventions that can be reproduced by different agents across different histories.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) trains multiple agents that coexist in a shared environment to make coordinated decisions, often leading to learned conventions such as role divisions. Machine unlearning is a subfield of machine learning focused on removing specific undesired elements (e.g., private data, outdated information, or dangerous abilities) from trained models without full retraining. Runtime shielding is a safety technique that blocks unsafe actions at execution time but leaves the underlying learned preferences intact, which can impair task progress. This paper addresses the gap that existing unlearning and trajectory-deletion methods do not directly target role-invariant coordination conventions in MARL.

## [Balanced Accuracy Can Mask Class Collapse in Neural-Signal Decoding Under Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17925) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper demonstrates that balanced accuracy, a common metric for imbalanced classification, can assign the same score to a decoder that reports only one class as to one that reports both classes at 50%, thereby failing to identify a class that has stopped being predicted under test-time adaptation. The study evaluated full test-time adaptation across 11 dataset-encoder cells using Siena/EEG, PTB-XL/ECG, and Sleep-EDF/EEG, finding that a minority class covering a tenth of a subject's samples went unpredicted in 26–47 of 112 subject-cells versus 7–9 for controls. This finding challenges the reliance on aggregate metrics like balanced accuracy for evaluating test-time adaptation in neural-signal decoding, showing that such scores can hide the complete deletion of a class and thus obscure clinically or scientifically meaningful failures. It argues for reporting class-level and subject-level changes alongside aggregate scores, which could affect how researchers and practitioners assess adaptation methods in EEG and ECG applications. The paper uses exact sign tests with Holm correction and bootstrap intervals, and notes that a stream-interleaving effect can reverse sign (+3.5 to -2.4 pp) depending on state sharing, bounding the protocol's conclusions. It also finds that aggregate and within-subject class contrast metrics named different encoders in 10 of 11 cells, and that only a few encoder pairs survive dependence-aware tests at nominal or fold-level references.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Nov 16, 05:00

**Background**: Test-time adaptation (TTA) refers to updating a model during inference using only incoming target-domain data, often without source labels, to handle distribution shifts. Balanced accuracy is a metric that averages per-class recall, designed to give equal weight to each class in imbalanced settings. Neural-signal decoding involves classifying physiological signals such as EEG (brain activity) and ECG (heart activity), where class imbalance and subject variability are common.

A new arXiv paper (2611.17950) shows that specialized RF source separators fail when applied to interference types they were not trained on, often leaving the signal worse than no processing. A unified separator that performs comparably to specialized models on seen pairs and matches the best specialized model on unseen interference could simplify deployment and improve robustness in real-world wireless communications. At the size of one specialized model, the unified separator keeps bit error rate close to the specialized models on almost every pair, better on one and worse on another. On an interference absent from all training sets, it performs as well as the best specialized model without needing to choose which one to deploy.
